Violent clashes have been ongoing in the northern part of [Myanmar](19457.md)'s [Rakhine State](493373.md) since October 2016. Insurgent attacks by the [Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army](52247136.md) (ARSA) have led to [sectarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarianism) violence perpetrated by Myanmar's military and the local [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Myanmar) population against predominantly [Muslim](6037917.md) [Rohingya](1918632.md) civilians.[^cite_note-satellite-20][^cite_note-MyanmarDeniesRape-21][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-aung_san_skip_20170913_npr_org-23] The conflict has sparked [international outcry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_Rohingya_genocide) and was described as an [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) by the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights.[^cite_note-aung_san_skip_20170913_npr_org-23][^cite_note-24] In August 2017, the situation worsened and hundreds of thousands of refugees fled Myanmar into [Bangladesh](3454.md), with an estimated 500,000 refugees having arrived by 27 September 2017.[^cite_note-Reuters928-25][^cite_note-Guardian928-26] In January 2019, [Arakan Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_Army_\(Kachin_State\)) insurgents raided border police posts in [Buthidaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung_Township),[^cite_note-27] joining the conflict and beginning their military campaign in northern Rakhine State against the Burmese military.[^cite_note-Lintner-28][^cite_note-Minar_2020-29]

The current conflict began on 9 October 2016 when ARSA insurgents attacked Burmese border posts along the [Bangladesh–Myanmar border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93Myanmar_border). In response, Burmese authorities launched "clearance operations" between October 2016 and June 2017, which killed more than 1,000 Rohingya civilians, according to UN officials.[^cite_note-bad_to_worse_2017_09_06_washpost-30][^cite_note-Independent2-31] Following attacks on military outposts by ARSA on 25 August 2017, sectarian violence erupted once again in northern Rakhine State. The Burmese military later claimed that 400 insurgents had died in the clashes that followed.[^cite_note-Reuters7-32][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34] However, the UN estimates that at least 1,000 people were killed between 25 August and 8 September.[^cite_note-un_secretary_gen_2017_09_13_cnn-35][^cite_note-Ben-36] By September, the violence had resulted in 389,000 Rohingyas fleeing their homes.[^cite_note-un_says_389000_fled_2017_09_14_ap-37]

A report published on 27 June 2018 by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) detailed [crimes against humanity](61099.md) perpetrated by Burmese military units both before and after 25 August 2017 ARSA attacks.[^cite_note-Quackenbush-38]

By February 2025, the ongoing [Rakhine offensive](76441089.md) launched by Arakan Army gained control of over 90% of Rakhine State, including 14 of its 17 townships.[^cite_note-\:1-39]

## Background

After Myanmar gained independence in 1948, [Rakhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakhine_people) and [Rohingya](1918632.md) nationalists both struggled to secure autonomy, as successive central governments continued to suppress their demands. In 2011 Myanmar's political reforms, allowed for greater ethnic expression, but these changes did not translate into real political power for the people.[^cite_note-\:0-40] In the 2015 elections, the [Arakan National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_National_Party) (ANP) won a majority of seats in Rakhine State, yet the central government bypassed them and appointed a chief minister from the ruling party. This move reinforced the belief among many Rakhine that electoral politics could not secure their rights, pushing some toward armed struggle.[^cite_note-\:0-40]

Amid these frustrations, the [Arakan Army](38106209.md) (AA), an armed group advocating for greater autonomy, gained popularity among the region. Founded in 2009, the AA positioned itself as the defender of Rakhine interests, leading to frequent clashes with the [Burmese military](66589972.md), especially from 2015 onward. The military responded with brutal crackdowns, including human rights violations, which fueled further resentment and resistance among the Rakhine population.[^cite_note-\:1-39]

In response to persistent oppression, [Rohingya](1918632.md) insurgent groups have formed over the years. Notably, the [Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army](52247136.md) (ARSA) emerged, claiming to defend Rohingya rights. ARSA's attacks on Myanmar security forces in 2016 and 2017 prompted brutal military crackdowns, resulting in mass atrocities against Rohingya civilians and the displacement of hundreds of thousands to neighboring Bangladesh. Other notable Rohingya insurgent groups include [Arakan Rohingya Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_Rohingya_Army), [Rohingya Solidarity Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_Solidarity_Organisation), and [Rohingya Islami Mahaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_Islami_Mahaz).[^cite_note-\:0-40][^cite_note-41]

In modern times, the [persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution) of Rohingyas in Myanmar dates back to the 1970s.[^cite_note-VOA-42] Since then, Rohingya people have regularly been made the target of persecution by the government and [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) [Buddhists](3267529.md).[^cite_note-GuardianUN-43] The tension between various religious groups in the country had often been exploited by the past military governments of Myanmar.[^cite_note-BBC-Who-44] According to [Amnesty International](18947898.md), the Rohingya have suffered from human rights violations under past [military dictatorships](146728.md) since 1978, and many have fled to neighbouring Bangladesh as a result.[^cite_note-Amn-45] In 2005, the [United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees](31964.md) had assisted with the repatriation of Rohingyas from Bangladesh, but allegations of human rights abuses in the refugee camps threatened this effort.[^cite_note-46] In 2015, 140,000 Rohingyas remained in [IDP](427408.md) camps after [communal riots in 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Rakhine_State_riots).[^cite_note-47]

## Rohingya insurgency



Northern Rakhine State settlements

### 2016–2017 clashes

#### 2016

On 9 October, hundreds of unidentified insurgents attacked three Burmese border posts along Myanmar's border with Bangladesh. According to government officials in the majority Rohingya border town of [Maungdaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maungdaw), the attackers brandished knives, machetes and homemade slingshots that fired metal bolts.[^cite_note-BBC2-48] Nine border officers were killed in the attack,[^cite_note-RFA1-49][^cite_note-BBC1-50][^cite_note-Reuters2-51] and 48 guns, 6,624 bullets, 47 bayonets and 164 bullet cartridges were looted by the insurgents.[^cite_note-52] Four Burmese soldiers were also killed two days later.[^cite_note-BBC3-53] Government officials in Rakhine State initially blamed the [Rohingya Solidarity Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_Solidarity_Organisation) (RSO), an Islamist insurgent group which was mainly active in the 1980s and 1990s, for the attacks,[^cite_note-AJ2-54] but a group calling itself [Harakah al-Yaqin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harakah_al-Yaqin) ("Faith Movement") later claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-BKP1-55]

Following the attacks in October, the [Myanmar Army](9503548.md) began "clearance operations"—widely seen as a military crackdown on the Rohingya—in the villages of northern Rakhine State. In the initial operation, dozens of people were killed and many were arrested.[^cite_note-Lady-CNN-56] The military enforced a curfew and blocked food aid from the [World Food Programme](33582.md) to 80,000 people in Rakhine State.[^cite_note-AJ19Oct2016-57] As the crackdown continued, the casualties increased. [Arbitrary arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_arrest), [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing), [gang rapes](1371871.md) and [looting](209811.md) were reportedly carried out by the military.[^cite_note-Amnesty-Vicious-58][^cite_note-Shoot-first-59][^cite_note-TI-UN-Kyi-60] Hundreds of Rohingya people had been killed by December 2016, and many had fled Myanmar as refugees to take shelter in the nearby areas of [Bangladesh](3454.md).[^cite_note-Lady-CNN-56][^cite_note-21000-rohingya-61][^cite_note-GuardianUN-43][^cite_note-Aljazeera-humanity-62]

The [chief of police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_police) in Rakhine State, Colonel [Sein Lwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sein_Lwin), announced on 2 November that his force had begun recruiting non-Rohingya locals for a new branch of "regional police", which would be trained in [Sittwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe), the capital of Rakhine State, and then sent back to their villages. The stated purpose of these forces was to defend villages against Rohingya insurgents; however, human rights organisations criticised the move, saying the new branch would likely to be used to further the persecution of Rohingya civilians.[^cite_note-Jazeera1-63][^cite_note-Reuters4-64]

New clashes began on 13 November, resulting in the deaths of one policeman, one soldier and six insurgents. Burmese police later arrested 36 suspected insurgents in connection to the attacks.[^cite_note-Reuters2-51][^cite_note-GD1-65][^cite_note-66] Fighting continued and on the third day of the fighting, the death toll rose to 134 (102 insurgents and 32 security forces). The government later announced that 234 people suspected of being linked to the attackers had been arrested.[^cite_note-Reuters1-67] Some of those arrested were later sentenced to death for their suspected involvement in the attack.[^cite_note-68]

#### 2017

Clashes between insurgents and the military continued in 2017. Burmese state media reported on 22 June that three insurgents had been killed by security forces during a two-day raid on an insurgent camp supposedly belonging to Harakah al-Yaqin, now calling themselves the Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army (ARSA). Authorities confiscated gunpowder, ski masks and wooden rifles suspected to have been used for training.[^cite_note-ASEAN1-69][^cite_note-70]

In February, the [United Nations](31769.md) accused Myanmar's military of mass killings and gang rapes, as well as burning down villages over the course of their "clearance operations". It stated that the military's operations had "likely caused hundreds of deaths."[^cite_note-71] Two senior UN officials working in its two separate agencies in Bangladesh stated that more than 1,000 may have died per testimonies gathered from Rohingyas. Meanwhile, Myanmar's presidential spokesman, Zaw Htay said that per latest reports from military commanders, fewer than 100 people had been killed.[^cite_note-72] The government of Myanmar denied that there was a systematic campaign of rape, though acknowledges it may have been committed by "individual members of the security forces".[^cite_note-MyanmarDeniesRape-21]

A police document obtained by *[Reuters](18998750.md)* in March listed 423 Rohingyas detained by the police since 9 October 2016, 13 of whom were children, the youngest being ten years old. Two police captains in Maungdaw verified the document and justified the arrests, with one of them saying, "We the police have to arrest those who collaborated with the attackers, children or not, but the court will decide if they are guilty; we are not the ones who decide." Myanmar police also claimed that the children had confessed to their alleged crimes during interrogations, and that they were not beaten or pressured during questioning. The average age of those detained is 34, the youngest is 10, and the oldest is 75.[^cite_note-Reuters3-73][^cite_note-Star1-74]

In April the Myanmar Army deployed an additional 1,000 soldiers to Rakhine State, increasing the number of active troops in the region to 2,000.[^cite_note-Lone-75]

Rakhine State police chief Sein Lwin announced on 27 June that security forces were on "high alert" after "masked assailants" killed several local administrators close to the Burmese government in the days prior.[^cite_note-76] A week later on 4 July, a mob of at least a hundred [Rakhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakhine_people) [Buddhists](3267529.md) in [Sittwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe) attacked seven Rohingya men from Dapaing camp for [internally displaced persons](427408.md) with bricks,[^cite_note-Independent-77] killing one and severely injuring another. The Rohingya men were being escorted by police to Sittwe's docks to purchase boats, but were attacked despite armed guards being present nearby.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] According to a spokesman for the [Burmese Ministry of Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_\(Myanmar\)), an unarmed junior policeman was with the Rohingya men at the time of the attack, but was unable to stop the attackers.[^cite_note-Independent-77] One man was arrested in relation to the attacks on 26 July.[^cite_note-81]

Bodies of slain locals have been discovered over the course of the conflict; the government has stated that they were victims of Rohingya insurgents. The bodies of three Rohingya locals who had worked closely with the local administration were found in shallow graves in Maungdaw on 21 January. The government suspected that they were murdered by Rohingya insurgents in a reprisal attack.[^cite_note-82] Three decapitated bodies were found in [Rathedaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathedaung_Township) on 31 July. According to a government official, they were murdered by Rohingya insurgents.[^cite_note-83] The bodies of six ethnic [Mro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mro_people_\(Awa_Khami\)) farmers, reportedly killed by Rohingya insurgents, were found in Maungdaw Township on 3 August.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86]

In mid-August 2017, Bangladesh security forces had stepped up its border patrols, following reports of 1,100 Rohingya migrating into Bangladesh in the past two weeks amid fresh tensions in Rakhine state. After the October 2016 clashes in Myanmar, the refugee flow had slowed until hundreds more soldiers were deployed recently.[^cite_note-87]

#### 25 August 2017 attacks

The government announced on 25 August that 71 people (one soldier, one immigration officer, 10 policemen and 59 insurgents) had been killed overnight during coordinated attacks by up to 150 insurgents on 24 police posts and the 552nd Light Infantry Battalion army base in Rakhine State.[^cite_note-BBC4-88][^cite_note-AP1-89][^cite_note-Reuters6-90] The Myanmar Army stated that the attack began at around 1\:00 am, when insurgents armed with bombs, light weapons and machetes blew up a bridge. The army further stated that a majority of the attacks occurred around 3\:00 am to 4\:00 am.[^cite_note-at_least_71_dead_2017_08_29_straits_times-91]

ARSA claimed they were taking "defensive actions" in 25 different locations and accused government soldiers of raping and killing civilians. The group also claimed that [Rathedaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathedaung) had been under a blockade for more than two weeks, starving the Rohingya, and that the government forces were preparing to do the same in Maungdaw.[^cite_note-92]

Myanmar's civilian leader, [Aung San Suu Kyi](2847.md), sharply criticised the insurgent Rohingya attacks as an attempt to undermine others' efforts to "build peace and harmony in Rakhine state."[^cite_note-pope_laments_2017_08_27_ap_foxnews-93] The United Nations condemned the attacks, while calling on all sides to refrain from violence.[^cite_note-94]

#### Military crackdown operations and violence

Following attacks by ARSA on 25 August, Myanmar's armed forces led a crackdown against Rohingyas in Rakhine State. The military referred to the action as "clearance operations",[^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95] but critics argued that the targets were Rohingya civilians and that it was an attempt to drive the Rohingya out of Myanmar.[^cite_note-myanmar_accused_2017_09_10_ap_chicago_tribune-96][^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97][^cite_note-TroopsOpenFire-98]

On 26 August, Myanmar troops allegedly opened fire on Rohingya civilians as they attempted to escape into Bangladesh.[^cite_note-TroopsOpenFire-98][^cite_note-DW-99]

The violence resulted in the deaths of hundreds of people in Myanmar's Rakhine state. At least 1,000 have been killed by 7 September, according to [Yanghee Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanghee_Lee), the United Nations Special Rapporteur on Human Rights for Myanmar. She added that the figure is "very likely an underestimate".[^cite_note-Ben-36] On the same day, Myanmar's armed forces claimed that 400 insurgents had been killed.[^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95][^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97] However, Rohingya accounts indicate that most of the dead were non-combatant civilians, including women and children.[^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97][^cite_note-bad_to_worse_2017_09_06_washpost-30]

Rohingya refugees quickly began fleeing Myanmar by the thousands,[^cite_note-satellite_data_2017_08_29_human_rights_watch-100][^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95] then, within two weeks, by the hundreds of thousands.[^cite_note-rohingya_have_fled_2017_09_08_ny_times-101][^cite_note-unhcr_reports_2017_09_08_ap_fox_news-102] By 12 September, authorities in Bangladesh and aid agencies were reporting 370,000 refugees had fled Myanmar, mostly Rohingya Muslims (about a third of the estimated Rohingya population in Myanmar),[^cite_note-textbook_example_2017_09_12_wash_post-103] but also hundreds of Hindus and Buddhists.[^cite_note-chicago_tribune_ap_26_dead_2017_08_31-104]

Rohingya refugees arrived in Bangladesh, many of whom had injuries such as bullet wounds, and reported fleeing indiscriminate shootings, rapes, torture and other violent acts against Rohingya civilians[^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95][^cite_note-myanmar_accused_2017_09_10_ap_chicago_tribune-96] by mobs of Buddhist Rakhine civilians and Myanmar government forces, including upon whole villages, many of which were burnt down[^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97] (reportedly some with villagers, even children, confined in the burning structures by their attackers).[^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95]

When [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) published satellite photos showing villages on fire,[^cite_note-satellite_data_2017_08_29_human_rights_watch-100][^cite_note-satellite_images_2017_09_02_human_rights_watch-105] the Burmese government acknowledged that Rohingya houses were burned, but again insisted (as they did in 2012) that the Rohingya insurgents were burning their own villages.[^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97][^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95] However, a group of reporters on a government-guided tour claimed they caught Rakhine civilians torching empty Rohingya houses, and one reporter alleged he was told by a Rakhine civilian that the police had assisted them.[^cite_note-new_fires_2017_09_07_ap_abc_news-95]

The U.N. High Commissioner for Human Rights said the violence appeared to be "a textbook example of 'ethnic cleansing.'"[^cite_note-textbook_example_2017_09_12_wash_post-103]

In late August 2017, Bangladesh proposed joint military operations with Myanmar against ARSA.[^cite_note-106] In September 2017, it accused Myanmar of repeatedly violating its [airspace](353557.md) and issued a warning stating that any more "provocative acts" could have "unwarranted consequences". Myanmar spokesman, Zaw Htay, stated Myanmar would check any information Bangladesh provided.[^cite_note-107]

On 2 September, the 33rd Light Infantry Division of the Myanmar Army and local paramilitaries [executed ten villagers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din_massacre) in [Inn Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din) who were suspected of being ARSA members.[^cite_note-Reuters92-108][^cite_note-Taylor-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111]

Starting on 6 September, reports emerged that the Myanmar Army had begun laying landmines near the border with Bangladesh.[^cite_note-MiningBorder-112] Bangladesh said it had photographic evidence of Myanmar laying mines,[^cite_note-AlJazeeraMines-113] and lodged an official complaint.[^cite_note-MiningBorder-112] On 10 September, Amnesty International accused Myanmar's security forces of laying landmines in violation of the [landmine ban under international law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_Treaty),[^cite_note-rohingyas_crisis_landmines_2017_09_12_bbc-114] an accusation Myanmar's government denied. The purpose of Myanmar laying mines is believed to be to prevent the Rohingya refugees from returning.[^cite_note-AlJazeeraMines-113] At least three people, including two children, had been injured in landmines as of 10 September.[^cite_note-myanmar_accused_2017_09_10_ap_chicago_tribune-96][^cite_note-rohingyas_crisis_landmines_2017_09_12_bbc-114] [Mro people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mro_people_\(Awa_Khami\)) crossing the border into Myanmar have also been injured by the mines.[^cite_note-115]

#### Ceasefire and aftermath



Members of the [Myanmar Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_Police_Force) patrolling in Maungdaw in September 2017.

A one-month unilateral ceasefire was declared by ARSA on 9 September, in an attempt to allow aid groups and humanitarian workers safe access into northern Rakhine State.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-JZceasefire-117][^cite_note-BBCceasefire-118] In a statement, the group urged the government to lay down their arms and agree to their ceasefire, which would have been in effect from 10 September until 9 October (the one-year anniversary of the first attacks on Burmese security forces by ARSA). The government rejected the ceasefire, with Zaw Htay, the spokesperson for the State Counsellor's office, stating, "We have no policy to negotiate with terrorists."[^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97][^cite_note-CNNceasefire-119]

The Myanmar government reported on 17 September that 176 of 471 Rohingya villages had been abandoned. By then it was reported that 430,000 Rohingyas had fled into neighbouring Bangladesh. The government of Bangladesh also announced plans to build shelters for 400,000 refugees and start immunisations, but began restricting the movement of some of the refugees.[^cite_note-bangladesh_restricts_2017_09_17_wash_post-120][^cite_note-myanmar_army_says_2017_09_17_bbc-121][^cite_note-we_will_kill_you_all_2017_09_17_reuters-122]

[United Nations Secretary General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Secretary_General) [António Guterres](471806.md) urged Myanmar State Counsellor Aung San Suu Kyi to use her planned speech to her nation on 19 September to announce a stop to the military offensive, warning that if she did not, "I do not see how this can be reversed in the future".[^cite_note-myanmar_army_says_2017_09_17_bbc-121] However, U.S. diplomats—while demanding that "this persecution must stop", attempted to be supportive of Suu Kyi's fragile, new civilian-leader position in a nation still largely ruled by the military.[^cite_note-myanmar_army_says_2017_09_17_bbc-121][^cite_note-bangladesh_restricts_2017_09_17_wash_post-120][^cite_note-we_will_kill_you_all_2017_09_17_reuters-122][^cite_note-myanmar_faces_2017_09_17_reuters-123][^cite_note-secretaries_2017_09_17_cspan_org-124] Suu Kyi's speech was later criticised by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) as "a mix of untruths and victim-blaming".[^cite_note-eco1-125] Doubt was also cast on her statement of there being no clashes after 5 September, with satellite imagery examined by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) appearing to show more than a dozen burned villages and fires since then.[^cite_note-126]

Myanmar's vice-president [Henry Van Thio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Van_Thio) told the UN on 20 September that Burmese security forces had been instructed to avoid collateral damage and harm to innocent civilians. He also promised that human rights violations would be dealt with "in accordance with strict norms of justice." Thio added that minority groups other than the Rohingya had also fled the violence and that his government was concerned over Rohingyas fleeing despite there being no armed clashes since 5 September.[^cite_note-127]

Rakhine Chief Minister [Nyi Pu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyi_Pu) and [Minister for Social Welfare, Relief and Resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_Welfare,_Relief_and_Resettlement) [Win Myat Aye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Win_Myat_Aye) visited the villages of Nyaung Pin Gyi and Ah Nauk Pyin to deliver aid after reports of Rohingya Muslims being trapped in the latter. An official also stated that those trapped in the villages were guaranteed safe passage by the two ministers, after local Rakhine Buddhists threatened them. Win Myat Aye stated that he had learned of the situation in the villages from international reports and that he and Nyi Pu had assured the Rohingya that they would take action against those intimidating them.[^cite_note-128]

ARSA stated on 7 October that they would respond to any peace initiatives proposed by Myanmar's government, but added that their one-month unilateral ceasefire was about to end.[^cite_note-129] Despite the ceasefire ending on 9 October, the government stated that there were no signs of any new attacks as it held inter-faith prayers in a stadium in [Yangon](57404.md) to curb animosity.[^cite_note-130]

Myanmar's military launched an internal probe into the actions of its soldiers during the August clashes.[^cite_note-131] In November, the military released a report denying all accusations against its security forces. Major General Maung Maung Soe was meanwhile transferred from his post as head of Western Command in Rakhine State in the same month.[^cite_note-Lone_2017-6]

While welcoming [Pope Francis](1687680.md) on 28 November during the second day of his visit to Myanmar, Aung San Suu Kyi stated that there had been an "erosion of trust and understanding" between the communities of Rakhine State.[^cite_note-132] The [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) stated on 13 December that an estimated 300,000 Rohingya Muslims remained in Rakhine State, 180,000 of whom were displaced in the north.[^cite_note-133] Human Rights Watch stated on 18 December that it had identified 40 Rohingya villages which had been destroyed in October and November.[^cite_note-BdnewsDec-134]

### Low-level insurgency (2017–present)

#### Late 2017

The state-run [*Global New Light of Myanmar*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Light_of_Myanmar) stated on 17 November that three trucks were extensively damaged in landmine explosions in [Minbya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minbya) two days earlier. It stated they were targeting army convoys, and added that another landmine exploded near a village later in the day as seven military trucks passed by, injuring a pedestrian. It also separately stated that 19 people had been arrested over the August attacks and charged under the anti-terrorism law.[^cite_note-135]

#### 2018

The Tatmadaw estimated the size of ARSA to be around 200 fighters in January 2018.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

ARSA claimed responsibility for an ambush carried out on a Burmese military convoy on 5 January in the village of Turaing, claiming they were fighting against "Burmese [state-sponsored terrorism](317645.md)" against Rohingyas. Six security personnel and a civilian driver were reportedly wounded[^cite_note-RFA31118-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] by gunfire and homemade [land mines](18172.md).[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

In mid-March, Burmese authorities began sponsoring the migration of [Rakhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakhine_people) [Buddhists](3267529.md) from Rakhine State's poorer southern regions to areas in the north that were formerly dominated by Rohingya Muslims. The village of [Inn Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din), which was the site of a [mass execution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din_massacre) of Rohingyas, had 250 new Rakhine arrivals from the south by 16 March.[^cite_note-144] According to Amnesty International, three facilities were being constructed for the security forces at the time, and Rohingyas were forcibly evicted from their village so that the land could be used for a military base. Four undamaged mosques were also reportedly dismantled.[^cite_note-Lewis_2018-145] Yanghee Lee stated on 12 March that the government of Myanmar appeared to be deliberately starving the Rohingya to make them flee.[^cite_note-146]

On 27 June, Amnesty International published a report on the situation in Rakhine State, detailing abuses faced by Rohingyas both before and after the August 2017 attacks. The report noted that the 33rd and 99th Light Infantry Divisions, known for committing abuses in the past, were sent to Rakhine State only weeks before the attacks happened, suggesting that the ensuing military crackdown was planned in advance. Thirteen military officials were also implicated in the report as being the primary sanctioners of the crackdown.[^cite_note-Quackenbush-38]

Myanmar's military announced in late December that it would resume "clearance operations" in northern Rakhine State after one policeman and three Rakhine Buddhist civilians were found murdered shortly after they were reported missing.[^cite_note-Guardian231218-147][^cite_note-148] Two [Maramagyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barua_\(Bangladesh\)) fishermen were also kidnapped and stabbed by six unidentified assailants speaking "Bengali", but they managed to escape their abductors according to Myanmar's commander-in-chief [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md).[^cite_note-149]

#### 2019

[Myanmar Radio and Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_Radio_and_Television) stated on 20 January that 10 members of ARSA attacked a post of the [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) near Maungdaw Township's Wet Kyein village on 16 January, leaving six policemen wounded.[^cite_note-150]

Two border guard policemen and an engineer of the Military Engineer Corps were wounded by gunfire near a border guard post near Wai Lar Taung village in Maungdaw Township according to a Tatmadaw spokesman on 24 January. The spokesman claimed the unidentified attackers fired at them from Bangladesh.[^cite_note-151] The [General Administration Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Administration_Department) however blamed the attack on ARSA. It cited a police report stating that 40 ARSA insurgents had attacked the post in order to capture it, but were forced to flee by the officers who received help from another regiment.[^cite_note-152]

A mine attack on a police vehicle traveling between villages of Muangdaw Township was conducted by ARSA on 22 April according to the Tatmadaw, injuring its driver.[^cite_note-153] Two security personnel were killed in an ambush of a border outpost by unknown gunmen from across Bangladesh on 7 July. Brig. Gen. Zaw Min Tun told *[Radio Free Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia)* that the area from where the attack occurred was controlled by ARSA and the group was suspected.[^cite_note-154] Two ARSA members were killed and three arrested on 21 November in Muangdaw, according to the Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-155]

#### 2020

A sub-lieutenant of the Tatmadaw and three civilian contractors were killed in March 2020, after their vehicle transporting materials for building a border fence hit an ARSA mine.[^cite_note-resurgence-156] Two people were killed and three injured after ARSA exploded motorbikes of 12 [Daingnets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daingnet_people) and shot at them near Bawtala Village in Maungdaw Township on 30 March, according to the state media.[^cite_note-157]

The Tatmadaw stated that it had seized ammunition and military equipment from an ARSA camp in the village of Kha Maung Seik on 8 April. Later, it stated that two policemen were killed in the village after being ambushed by the group on 15 April. Two members of the group were found dead with improvised landmines on 29 April according to it.[^cite_note-Lwin-158]

A [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) unit was attacked by ARSA insurgents near the [Bangladesh–Myanmar border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93Myanmar_border) on 2 May according to *[Myawady Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myawady_Daily)*, injuring two officers.[^cite_note-159] According to the military, the insurgents retreated after it arrived to assist the police force.[^cite_note-Lwin-158]

Two alleged ARSA insurgents were killed in a confrontation with Myanmar security forces at the Bangladesh–Myanmar border on 4 June near Mee Dike village, according to the Tatmadaw, which stated that around 30 insurgents opened fire on a routine border patrol by the border police and retreated after 30 minutes of fighting. Some policemen were also injured per the military.[^cite_note-160]

Three residents of Khayay Myaing village were killed and six others wounded when their vehicle struck a mine in Maungdaw Township on 17 November. [Maungdaw District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maungdaw_District) administrator Soe Aung stated that the government believed that ARSA was behind the blast.[^cite_note-161] In middle of 2020, an Islamist insurgent group [Islami Mahaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_Islami_Mahaz) claimed responsibility for several attacks against Myanmar. Islami Mahaz was also responsible for killing of people in refugee camp who were allegedly supporters of [ARSA](52247136.md).[^cite_note-162]

### During the Myanmar civil war (2021–present)

For broader coverage of this topic, see [Myanmar civil war (2021–present)](69807451.md).

The [Rohingya Solidarity Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_Solidarity_Organization) (RSO) claimed to have killed 22 Myanmar Army soldiers on 30 March 2021 after attacking a military camp in Rakhine State.[^cite_note-163] The RSO re-emerged amidst the clashes in Rakhine State, and started targeting people who supported ARSA.[^cite_note-RSO-164] [Rohingya Islami Mahaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_Islami_Mahaz) allied with RSO following the rearmament of the group.[^cite_note-165]

The conflict also allowed another armed Rohingya group named [Arakan Rohingya Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_Rohingya_Army) (ARA), led by Abdullah Kane and reportedly consisting of former RSO members, to emerge. In May, a furniture seller and a former village administrator were kidnapped and robbed. A village administrator of Padin village also suffered the same treatment in August. An administrator of Pan Taw Pyin village in Maungdaw was killed on 2 September, while two others were wounded. The Rohingya Nationalist Organization, based in the [Kutupalong refugee camp](54548485.md), accused Kane's group of being behind the attacks.[^cite_note-RSO-164][^cite_note-166]

ARSA started re-emerging in Maungdaw District around November 2021, after allying with the [National Unity Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Unity_Government) formed in opposition to the [military junta](66589972.md) that took power after the [2021 Myanmar coup d'état](66567997.md). The group clashed with the military in Maungdaw during November. Maung Hla, the administrative officer of Khone Taing village, stated that their base was located in Wela hills, about 20 miles (32 km) north of Maungdaw.[^cite_note-Wela-167]

On 7 December, 23 residents of Khone Taing village were shot at by ARSA near La Baw Chaung mountain, located around 2 miles (3.2 km) from the village, injuring one of them.[^cite_note-resurgence-156]

Several images and videos appeared on social media in early-January 2022, purportedly showing more than 50 members of the group along with its leaders taking part in a military drill in Maungdaw.[^cite_note-Wela-167] The group clashed with the [Arakan Army](38106209.md) on 19 July 2023 in the Mayu mountains near Sein Hnyin Pyar and Gu Dar Pyin villages. The AA claimed that five ARSA members and one AA member were killed, with the AA seizing an ARSA outpost.[^cite_note-168] ARSA was accused of killing a villager of Meekyaungkaungswe village in Buthidaung Township on 10 September.[^cite_note-169]

The ARA remerged under Nabi Hossain in February 2024, with a purported video of Nabi showing off weapons and ammunition seized from the Border Guard Police on 5 February and asking Rohingyas to join the struggle against the Tatmadaw being released on social media. Witnesses later stated that the group had seized a BGP camp in Dhekibuniya, but were forced to flee to Bangladesh after they were attacked by Arakan Army and the RSO on the following day.[^cite_note-170]

ARSA meanwhile began cooperating with the Tatmadaw in 2024. It was also accused of killing two civilians in Taung Pyo Let Wae town of [Maungdaw Township](15160625.md) on 7 March 2024. Local residents then urged the Arakan Army to start "clearance operations" against ARSA.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-Fools-172] On 12 April, clashes broke out between the Arakan Army and the junta-supported fighters of ARSA based in U Hla Hpay, Ywet Nyo Taung and Kun Taing villages of [Buthidaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung_Township).[^cite_note-173] ARSA and ARA were also accused of burning down homes and kidnapping civilians in Buthidaung Township.[^cite_note-174]

The RSO and the ARA were reported in May 2024 to be forcibly recruiting Rohingyas from refugee camps in Bangladesh for both the Tatmadaw and Arakan Army. The RSO for its part claimed that it was only apprehending its own members and that of the ARA. Arakan Army spokesman Khaing Thuka meanwhile criticized the two groups as well as ARSA for their recruitment practices in camps in Bangladesh.[^cite_note-175]

ARSA launched a large scale attack against camps and outposts of the Arakan Army in the town of Taungpyoletwea of Maungdaw Township on 10 and 11 August 2025.[^cite_note-176] On 22 September, it claimed to have captured a main base of the Arakan Army in the Maungdaw Township, the AA however denied the claim, stating that ARSA did attack a border outpost but retreated into Bangladesh after a counterattack.[^cite_note-177]

The Development Media Group stated that 40 Rakhine civilians had been killed and 17 injured in ARSA attacks from May 2025 to 23 October 2025.[^cite_note-178] By late-November 2025, fighting between the Arakan Army and the Rohingya militant groups ARSA and RSO were reported to have intensified.[^cite_note-179]

## Arakan Army insurgency

Further information: [Rakhine offensive (2023–present)](76441089.md)

### 2018

Clashes in [Buthidaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung_Township) and [Rathedaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathedaung_Township) between the Arakan Army, an ethnic [Rakhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakhine_people) insurgent group, and the Tatmadaw, killed four soldiers and an unknown number of Arakan Army insurgents according to *[Myawady Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myawady_Daily)*. It also stated that army men with senior ranks were killed, but did not provide any details. The clash started on 3 December and continued till 5 December according to Myawady, after it was ambushed during area-clearance operations near the border with Bangladesh. Arakan Army said that the conflict occurred after Tatmadaw entered territory controlled by them.[^cite_note-180] The clashes intensified by the next week, forcing 330 people to flee from Buthidaung.[^cite_note-181] The Arakan Army had earlier clashed with Myanmar's Army in April 2015.[^cite_note-182]

Rathedaung, [Ponnagyun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponnagyun_Township), [Kyauktaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyauktaw_Township), Buthidaung and [Paletwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paletwa_Township) townships meanwhile experienced clashes between Arakan Army and Tatmadaw throughout December. The Arakan Army accused Tatmadaw of using heavy weapons as well as helicopters in Paletwa, but Zaw Htay rejected usage of helicopters in fighting. Villagers meanwhile accused Tatmadaw of detaining civilians and stated over 2,000 people had fled due to the clashes.[^cite_note-183]

### 2019

#### January Arakan Army attacks

The government-owned [*Global New Light of Myanmar*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Light_of_Myanmar) reported on 1 January that 30 insurgents of the Arakan Army had attacked members of the [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) (BGP) that day in Bathidaung Township's Saytaung village, leaving one policeman gravely wounded. The Arakan Army denied responsibility, but admitted it had clashed with other members of Myanmar's security forces.[^cite_note-184] On 4 January, around 300 members of the Arakan Army launched pre-dawn [attacks on four border police outposts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Buthidaung_raids)—Kyaung Taung, Nga Myin Taw, Ka Htee La and Kone Myint—in northern [Buthidaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung_Township).[^cite_note-Lintner-28] Thirteen members of the [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) (BGP) were killed and nine others were injured,[^cite_note-strait5119-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] whilst 40 firearms and more than 10,000 rounds of ammunition were looted. The Arakan Army later stated that it had captured nine BGP personnel and five civilians, and that three of its fighters were also killed in the attacks.[^cite_note-WSJ4119-188][^cite_note-irr5119-189] Following the attacks, the Office of the [President of Myanmar](11195083.md) held a high-level meeting on national security in the capital [Naypyidaw](2270051.md) on 7 January, and instructed the Defense Ministry to increase troop deployments in the areas that were attacked and to use aircraft if necessary.[^cite_note-190]

#### Myanmar Army's response and subsequent clashes

Myanmar Army soldiers from the 22nd Light Infantry Division, elements of the 66th and 99th Light Infantry Divisions, and other battalions from the Western Command were reportedly involved in the subsequent military offensive against the Arakan Army. Clashes were reported in [Maungdaw](15160625.md), [Buthidaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung_Township), [Kyauktaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyauktaw_Township), [Rathedaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathedaung_Township) and [Ponnagyun Townships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponnagyun_Township), located in the northern and central parts of Rakhine State. The Myanmar Army claimed it killed 13 Arakan Army insurgents in military operations between 5 and 16 January.[^cite_note-AFP19119-191]

Immediately after the initial attacks, the [Rakhine State government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakhine_State_Government) issued a notice blocking non-governmental organisations and UN agencies, except for the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) and the [World Food Programme](33582.md), from travelling to rural areas in these townships affected by the conflict. The fighting prompted 5,000 civilians to flee from their homes and to take shelter in monasteries and communal areas across the region, according to the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs.[^cite_note-192] Civilian casualties,[^cite_note-193] arbitrary detention of ethnic Rakhine villagers,[^cite_note-194] looting of homes,[^cite_note-195] and military blockage of food aid and medical relief were also reported.[^cite_note-196]

A mine explosion killed two police officers and injured two others in [Ponnagyun Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponnagyun_Township) on 27 February, after which the police convoy they were travelling with came under gunfire. The police blamed the attack on Arakan Army.[^cite_note-197] From September 2018 to February 2019, about four people believed to be informants were killed, leading to suspicion that they were being targeted by Arakan Army and Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-198]

Around 60 Arakan Army insurgents launched an evening attack on Yoe-ta-yoke Police Station on 9 March. According to a leaked combat report, nine policemen were killed, two were injured, and a dozen weapons, including 10 BA-63 battle rifles, were stolen by the attackers.[^cite_note-199] On the same day, Arakan Army insurgents managed to conquer the front line commanding post of Rakhine State's Gwa Township-based No. 563 Light Infantry Battalion under the supervision of Light Infantry Division No. 5. According to a press release by the Arakan Army, 11 personnel, including four military engineers, were captured and 16 backhoe excavators, one Toyota car, a dump truck, and 60 mm and 80 mm mortars were confiscated.[^cite_note-200]

About 400 villagers fled fighting in Mrauk U from 16 to 17 March. The Arakan Army, however, claimed that the military was staging false battles and firing upon living areas of civilians. The clashes in the town meanwhile had damaged archaeological buildings.[^cite_note-201] Six civilians were injured on 18 March according to residents of the township during firing. The military claimed that its convoy was ambushed by the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-202] Residents of the town, however, said that they indiscriminately targeted civilian areas and did not clash with any rebels.[^cite_note-203]

Two police officers and a wife of one of the officers were killed in an attack by 200 members of Arakan Army on a police base in Mrauk U on 9 April. An Arakan Army spokesman, however, denied any civilians were harmed, claiming the base was being used to store heavy weapons only. Residents meanwhile reported that the military was using warplanes.[^cite_note-204] During clashes between Arakan Army and Tatmadaw on 21 May killed two Rohingyas of [Kyauktaw Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyauktaw_Township)'s Alae Gyun village when their home was hit by a shell.[^cite_note-205] A battle in Minbya Township that started on 2 June left casualties on both sides. Four civilians were also killed and six injured when a shell fell on a monastery of Sapar Htar village the next day.[^cite_note-206]

The government of Myanmar announced authorisation for the Tatmadaw to use helicopters against insurgents on 7 June.[^cite_note-207] Battles between Arakan Army and the military near Minya township's Shwegyin village and Ghahtar Taung Dam in [Mrauk-U township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mrauk-U_Township) caused hundreds of villagers to flee in late June.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] Clashes between two sides occurred in Kyauktaw and Rathedaung townships on 8 August, leaving casualties on both sides.[^cite_note-210]

The Arakan Army attacked a border police post in Nyaungchaung village of Buthidaung township on 26 July, with two policemen and four villagers being wounded in the resulting clashes.[^cite_note-211] A mine attack on a police convoy by the Arakan Army killed three policemen on 20 August near Pat Kwet village per the [Myanmar Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_Police_Force).[^cite_note-212] On 23 August, airplanes were called into action against Arakan Army in Minbya Township. Meanwhile, the clashes since January had left over 100 civilians dead.[^cite_note-213] On 28 August, the rebels attacked a base of Tatmadaw in Mrauk-U.[^cite_note-214] A battle between the Tatmadaw and Arakan Army saw the latter temporarily capture their artillery base in [Myebon Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myebon_Township) on 24 September.[^cite_note-215]

On 26 October, a group of 30 Arakan Army insurgents boarded a civilian ferry and kidnapped 58 off-duty soldiers and policemen.[^cite_note-Gregory-216] The next day, three boats carrying the hostages were attacked by government helicopters, sinking two and resulting in the deaths of several insurgents and hostages.[^cite_note-Gregory-216][^cite_note-NangIves-217] Fifteen of the hostages were later rescued.[^cite_note-NangIves-217] On 3 November, the Arakan Army abducted ten people from a speedboat which included a [Member of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_\(Myanmar\)) belonging to [National League for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_for_Democracy) (NLD) and five Indian workers. An Indian died the next day which the Arakan Army stated was due to fatigue while walking. Everyone were released later except the lawmaker, whom the Arakan Army said was their real target.[^cite_note-218] The MP was released in January 2020.[^cite_note-219]

A Rohingya Muslim civilian was killed during firing between Arakan Army and Tatmadaw in Phone Nyo Leik village of Buthidaung Township on 6 November 2019. Meanwhile 35 houses of Thayatpyin village were burnt in the shelling.[^cite_note-220] Nearly 1,700 civilians fled from three townships fearing the army's clearance operations in the next week.[^cite_note-221] The Arakan Army accused the military of causing the death of NLD's Buthidaung Township chairman Ye Thein after attacking it on 25 December. The Tatmadaw, however, denied that any such clashes took place in Buthidaung and the NLD blamed the Arakan Army for the death.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

### 2020

On 13 February, at least 19 Rakhine children were wounded due to shelling that struck a school of Khamwe Chaung village in Buthidaung Township. Both the Tatmadaw and Arakan Army blamed each other for the shelling.[^cite_note-224] Five civilians were injured due to shelling on Pike The and Let Saung Kauk villages of Kyauktaw Township on 11 May after a military truck was attacked. The Tatmadaw blamed the Arakan Army of attacking them, but stated that there was no clash in the township.[^cite_note-225]

On 29 May, the Arakan Army ambushed a border police outpost and killed four policemen in Thazin Myaing village of Rathedaung Township according to the Tatmadaw. The Arakan Army confirmed the attack and stated it was in retaliation for the Tatmadaw attacking their medical facility in [Chin State](493366.md)'s [Paletwa Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paletwa_Township) earlier. Six policemen and three civilians were also kidnapped, but the civilians were later released.[^cite_note-226] *[The Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irrawaddy)* reported in July 2020 that three [Arakan Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_Liberation_Army) members who were abducted by Arakan Army in May were later killed according to a party source.[^cite_note-227]

A military column on the outskirts of [Ponnagyun Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponnagyun_Township) was attacked by the Arakan Army with a remotely detonated mine on 2 June according to the Tatmadaw, leading to the death of a civilian and an unknown number of soldiers.[^cite_note-228] The group later attacked vessels of the Myanmar navy near Amyint Kyun village of [Sittwe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe_Township) on 10 June according to residents and the Tatmadaw, leading to the navy returning fire. The resulting shelling killed a civilian and injured four people.[^cite_note-229] The Tatmadaw stated that on 13 June that the Arakan Army had attacked the Done Pike border guard outpost in Rathedaung Township and injured some police officers.[^cite_note-230] Later on 23 June, it stated that three border guards and a civilian were killed after an ambush by the Arakan Army a day earlier near Kotankauk village in Rathedaung Township.[^cite_note-231]

Thousands of civilians fled after local governments informed 40 villages of clearance operations in late June, but the Burmese military later stated that operations would only be carried out in five villages.[^cite_note-232] MP [Khin Maung Latt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khin_Maung_Latt) said on 29 June that 10,000 people had left their villages since the issuance of the order.[^cite_note-233] It was later revoked by the state government which said that residents had misunderstood the notification.[^cite_note-234] By July 2020, the Arakan Army had started collecting taxes and policing areas, following up on its announcement in December 2019 about setting up a "Rakhine People's Authority".[^cite_note-235]

The village chief of Ton Ma Wa in Paletwa Township was found dead on 17 July, after being abducted by the Arakan Army on 5 June. The Khumi Affairs Coordination Council accused the group of killing him.[^cite_note-236] Tatmadaw officials reported about the insurgents ambushing soldiers, policemen and government officials between 3 and 4 August, resulting in casualties on the government side as well as the death of a government official.[^cite_note-237] The [Arakan Liberation Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_Liberation_Party) accused the group of abducting five of its members near Myaung Gyi village of Ponnagyun Township on 10 August.[^cite_note-238] They were released on 18 August.[^cite_note-239]

On 4 September, Tatmadaw spokesman Zaw Min Tun accused the Arakan Army of exploding landmines near a police convoy escorted by the military in Kyauktaw Township. He also denied reports claiming the Tatmadaw had burned down the villages of Phayar Paung and Taung Pauk, and further stated that two soldiers had been found dead. The Arakan Army, however, rejected mounting any attack, claimed the two deceased were civilians, and accused the Tatmadaw of fabricating narratives as an excuse for their arson on the two villages. The accounts of arson by the military were also reported by locals, who also stated that two civilians had been killed.[^cite_note-240]

Chin State's Municipal Minister Soe Htet and eleven government officials accompanying him were caught in an attack by the Arakan Army on 9 September, forcing them to return to Paletwa. The Tatmadaw's True News Information stated that the group had attacked a military base and not him.[^cite_note-241]

About 3,000 were displaced on 23 September during military operations near Gutaung village of Rathedaung Township.[^cite_note-242] Clashes intensified in Rathedaung on 3 October, with the military bringing in jet planes and the [Myanmar Navy](9502915.md) as it engaged in clashes with the Arakan Army for control of a strategic hill near Aungtharzi village.[^cite_note-243] The military claimed to have taken the hill on 4 October, but the Arakan Army stated on the following day that it had retaken it.[^cite_note-244] Fighting also took place near Kyauktan village of Rathedaung Township, with the military calling in fighter jets to bomb the group.[^cite_note-245]

The Arakan Army abducted three NLD candidates from [Taunggok Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taungup_Township) on 14 October, with a witness alleging that they also beat up 11 people. It demanded the release of student protesters and other civilians who had been arrested, in exchange for the release of the candidates.[^cite_note-246] The group later struck a temporary outpost of the Tatmadaw at Mount Mayu near [Inn Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din) village and an outpost of the [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) near Ahtet Nan Yar village of Rathedaung Township on 26 October. The military's spokesman Major General Zaw Min Tun stated that they had suffered casualties, without specifying the death toll.[^cite_note-247]

The Arakan Army announced a unilateral ceasefire in late November so that voting for the [2020 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Myanmar_general_election), which had earlier been cancelled in nine townships in northern Rakhine State, could be held by the end of the year.[^cite_note-248] The Tatmadaw also entered into dialogue, with clashes ceasing after the election.[^cite_note-249]

### 2021

On 1 January 2021, following negotiations with the Tatmadaw, the Arakan Army released the NLD candidates it had abducted in October 2020, along with three soldiers. The Arakan Army also called for the release of Rakhine politicians, civilians and its own members.[^cite_note-250] Despite both the Tatmadaw and Arakan Army backing elections in northern Rakhine, the NLD government later expressed reluctance in holding it stating there were no security guarantees by either side.[^cite_note-251]

The [State Administration Council](66589972.md) military junta installed following the [February 2021 coup d'état](66567997.md) removed the Arakan Army from Myanmar's list of terrorist groups on 11 March, citing the organisation's decision in late 2020 to halt its attacks and in order to encourage peace.[^cite_note-252] The [Northern Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alliance_\(Myanmar\)) consisting of the Arakan Army, [Ta'ang National Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta'ang_National_Liberation_Army) and [Myanmar National Democratic Alliance Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_National_Democratic_Alliance_Army), however, warned on 30 March it would join the protestors in opposing the coup if the military did not stop attacking them, putting the ceasefire at risk.[^cite_note-253]

The Arakan Army's leader [Twan Mrat Naing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twan_Mrat_Naing) stated in April 2021 that he did not want the [anti-coup protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Myanmar_protests) to spread to Rakhine State, believing it may jeopardise negotiations between his forces and the Tatmadaw. However, he also expressed sympathy for the civilians killed by the Tatmadaw, and promised to "help every ethnic group achieve their political goals".[^cite_note-254] As part of peace efforts, 10 ethnic Rakhine prisoners, including three relatives of Naing, were cleared of all charges relating to seizure of explosives in [Mandalay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandalay) and released on 9 June.[^cite_note-255]

Six military officers captured in clashes were released by the Arakan Army on 16 June, in Kyauk Kyak village of Mrauk-U Township. A day later, 17 policemen and soldiers abducted from the Shwe Nadi ferry on the Mayu River in October 2019 were released by the group near the Taintaungpyin village in Buthidaung township.[^cite_note-256]

On 27 June, the Arakan Army disarmed more than 20 policemen stationed at a temporary outpost in the Mahamuni Buddha Temple in Kyauktaw Township, after surrounding it. The stationing of the police forces at the temple had been a source of contention. The group's spokesman later stated that the situation had been resolved peacefully.[^cite_note-257]

The Arakan Army and its political wing [United League of Arakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_League_of_Arakan) (ULA) started asserting administrative control over the region since the coup, issuing stay-at-home orders on 20 July in response to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), in addition to announcing the setting up of a dispute resolution mechanism and asking civilians to report crimes to the ULA on 1 August.[^cite_note-258] In response, the Tatmadaw started strengthening its troops numbers in the state, increasing inspections of homes at night, and maintaining a list of visitors from outside Rakhine.[^cite_note-259] Pe Than, a former MP of the [Pyithu Hluttaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyithu_Hluttaw) from [Myebon Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myebon_Township), told *[Radio Free Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Asia)* in September 2021 that the Arakan Army controlled 80% of the region while the junta's administrative system had collapsed.[^cite_note-260]

Clashes between the Arakan Army and the Tatmadaw broke out on 9 November, the first time since the ceasefire came in effect, to the north of Maungdaw. The group accused the Tatmadaw of provoking the fighting by entering an area controlled by it.[^cite_note-261] The military, however, denied it had clashed with them, stating that it had only fought ARSA.[^cite_note-262]

### 2022

Aung Thaung Shwe, a former MP of the Pyithu Hluttaw, told *Radio Free Asia* in January 2022 that people of Rakhine State were increasingly relying on the Arakan Army's judicial system and crime had significantly dropped since the group took over the state's judiciary. Pe Than estimated that the group controlled two-thirds of Rakhine and added that the military was finding it difficult to move freely in the state.[^cite_note-263]

Soldiers from the 352nd Light Infantry Battalion attacked an outpost of Arakan Army in Letpan mountains near Mee Taik village, located in Maungdaw, on 4 February. This resulted in the death of an Arakan Army member. The two sides clashed again on 6 February for three hours, and the Arakan Army ambushed a vehicle of the Tatmadaw on the following day. At least seven soldiers and two civilians were known to have been killed in the clashes. It later attacked two columns of the Tatmadaw in Wai Lar mountains in northern Maungdaw, with *[The Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irrawaddy)* stating that dozens of soldiers were killed and several captured. The clashes however ceased after negotiations between the two sides.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265]

The Arakan Army threatened to resume hostilities in April 2022, in response to the Tatmadaw's interference in ULA courts and opposition to the Arakan Army officials meeting with Rohingyas.[^cite_note-266] A brief clash took between the two sides in Wet Gaung village of Myebon Township on 12 April according to locals.[^cite_note-267] Meanwhile, the military forces started arresting people for being linked to the Arakan Army, something it had avoided since the beginning of the ceasefire. These arrests and inspections of Rakhine villages peaked in mid-April. On 6 May, the Arakan Army's chief Tun Myat Naing warned Maj. Gen. [Htin Latt Oo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Htin_Latt_Oo), commander of the Western Command of the Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-268]

A clash took place between the military and the Arakan Army near Than Htaung village of Paletwa Township on 15 May.[^cite_note-269] The group stated on the same day that renewed clashes could break out at any time, and urged civilians to avoid areas where the Myanmar Army was stationed.[^cite_note-270] The National Unity Government held talks with the Arakan Army for the first time on 16 May and referred to it as the "ULA/AA-led Rakhine People's Government".[^cite_note-271]

Clashes occurred between the military and the Arakan Army near Mount Suu Poke, located near Abaung Thar village of Paletwa Township, on 26 May and involved artillery.[^cite_note-272] The group abducted thirteen security personnel in Rakhine State in June 2022 in response to the military arresting its staff.[^cite_note-273] On 18 July, clashes broke out between the two sides in Maungdaw Township after the group attacked the Tatmadaw and the Border Guard in retaliation for the military killing six of its members while bombing its outpost in [Karen State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_State) on 4 July.[^cite_note-274] The group captured fourteen security personnel, while also claiming to have killed around twenty more.[^cite_note-275]

On 18 July, battles erupted between the two sides in three areas of Rathedaung and Muangdaw townships.[^cite_note-Summer2022clashes-276] On 24 July they clashed near Abaung Thar village,[^cite_note-277] while another clash took place in Maungdaw Township on 26 July.[^cite_note-Summer2022clashes-276] A battle between them began on 2 August near Namada village in Paletwa Township. The junta lost at least 14 soldiers and an unspecified number of Border Police members in the clashes.[^cite_note-boat-278] On the same day, the Arakan Army abducted ALP leader Lt. Col. Khaing Paw Lin.[^cite_note-279]

On 13 August, the Arakan Army and the Tatmadaw clashed in Paletwa, Rathedaung and Maungdaw townships. The group claimed that 37 soldiers were killed in the clashes.[^cite_note-Summer2022clashes-276] On 15 August, it attacked two hilltop outposts of Light Infantry Battalion 289 in Paletwa.[^cite_note-280] A clash took place near the Bangladesh–Myanmar border in Muangdaw Township on the following day.[^cite_note-281] A days-long battle erupted between the two sides near Ah Baung Thar, Hna Maung Dar and Ree Ma Wa villages of Paletwa Township on 21 August, forcing around 1,000 civilians to flee from the three villages.[^cite_note-282]

The Tatmadaw bombed suspected Arakan Army camps in northern Maungdaw Township on 25 August, while an attack by the group at a military camp located on the Kha Moung Seik Byuhar hill led to a battle breaking out between the two sides.[^cite_note-RFAAug2022-283] The Tatmadaw and the police were also forced to abandon a police station in Thang Htaung on the same day. On 27 August, the group attacked an outpost in Kha Maung Seik, with the Tatmadaw conducting airstrikes to counter them. On the following day, it ambushed a military column in Lekka village of Mrauk-U Township.[^cite_note-Aug2022-284]

More than 5,000 civilians from ten villages in Rathedaung Township were forced to flee on 30 August after a battle broke out between the two sides near Ma Nyin Taung village. The Arakan Army claimed that they clashed with the Tatmadaw near Myeik Wa village in Paletwa Township on the following day,[^cite_note-RFAAug2022-283] with ten soldiers being killed.[^cite_note-285] The group also captured a police outpost near the border with Bangladesh in Maungdaw Township on the same day, and claimed to have killed 19 policemen.[^cite_note-286] Overall it captured 30 military outposts in Rakhine State and six in Paletwa Township in August.[^cite_note-287]

Amidst the resumed fighting, Bangladesh expressed concerns over repeated violations of its airspace and artillery from Myanmar landing in its territory.[^cite_note-288] On 10 September, the Arakan Army captured an outpost of Infantry Battalion 352 located 3 kilometres (1.9 mi) from Jade Chaung village in Maungdaw Township. 13 soldiers were killed in the fighting according to *Western News*, while four were captured.[^cite_note-289] The group also attacked a hilltop base of the Tatmadaw near Temawa village in Paletwa Township on 15 September, leading to heavy clashes.[^cite_note-290] A Rohingya refugee was killed and six others were injured on the following day when shells fired from Myanmar landed inside the territory of Bangladesh.[^cite_note-291]

The Arakan Army captured the Lake Ya outpost near the border with Bangladesh in Maungdaw Township on 10 October, killing 30 soldiers and capturing three.[^cite_note-292] Four soldiers were killed in Rathedaung Township on 26 October, while in Buthidaung Township 11 were killed and more than 15 were injured on 28 October according to the Arakan Army. Clashes occurred in Kyauktaw, Mrauk-U and Paletwa townships on 29 October, while the group claimed to have killed five soldiers of Light Infantry Battalion 374 on the following day. The group later stated that three soldiers were killed and ten were wounded near Thayargone village in Maungdaw Township on 31 October when Light Infantry Battalion 201 troops entered its territory.[^cite_note-293]

The Arakan Army claimed to have killed at least three soldiers in an ambush near Ywa Pakar village in Taungup Township on 1 November. Three Border Guard members were also killed and six others were injured. Several soldiers were killed in a mine attack on a military convoy near Kywe Tet Pyin village in Maungdaw Township on 2 November. Fighting also broke out near Myeik Wa Village in Paletwa Township on the same day.[^cite_note-294] Based on local news reports and statements by the Arakan Army, *[The Diplomat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Diplomat_\(magazine\))* identified that more than 100 battles had occurred in the state between July and November 2022.[^cite_note-Diplomat-295]

On 26 November, the Arakan Army and the junta agreed to a temporary ceasefire beginning on the following day. It was brokered by [Yōhei Sasakawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C5%8Dhei_Sasakawa) of the [Nippon Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Foundation) who acted as an intermediary. AA spokseman Khine Thu Kha stated that they agreed to it for humanitarian reasons and not because of international pressure. The group did not withdraw from fortifications held at the time of the ceasefire.[^cite_note-ceasefirerfa-296] A junta official told *[The Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irrawaddy)* that it was the first step towards a permanent ceasefire.[^cite_note-297] As of mid-December, tensions remained high with forces from both sides remaining in deployment within northern Rakhine State.[^cite_note-298]

### 2023

Three commanders of the [Arakan Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arakan_Liberation_Army) were assassinated in Sittwe on 4 January 2023, including the commander-in-chief Khaing Soe Mya and the commander of Battalion 101 Khaing Kyaw Soe. The group blamed the Arakan Army, which denied any involvement.[^cite_note-299] On 1 July, three more ALP members were assassinated in Sittwe, including ALP vice chairman Khaing Ni Yaung, Battalion 1 commander Khaing Kyaw Min, and a private. The ALP accused the AA of being behind it, with the group rejecting the charge.[^cite_note-300]

The Arakan Army launched attacks against the outposts of the [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) in Rathedaung Township as part of [Operation 1027](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_1027) on 13 November 2023,[^cite_note-301] before expanding their attacks on junta forces to other towns. According to AA reports, the junta forces had abandoned almost 40 outposts by the next day, leading to 34 junta troops and six AA fighters being killed.[^cite_note-302] It captured [Pauktaw Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauktaw_Township) on 16 November,[^cite_note-303] however the Tamatdaw retook it the same day[^cite_note-304] By 23 November, the group had captured ten bases.[^cite_note-305] On 14 December, it stated that it had captured 142 military positions during the offensive.[^cite_note-306] The group captured the Taw Ma Taung military camp in Mrauk-U Township on 25 December and the [Mrauk-U District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mrauk-U_District) police station on the following day.[^cite_note-police-307]

### 2024

On 8 January 2024, the Arakan Army captured the Taung Shey Taung base in Kyauktaw Township after the surrender of about 200 soldiers. Meanwhile, they escalated their offensive into Paletwa Township as part of Operation 1027, and claimed to have captured all junta bases in west of Paletwa with 255 junta soldiers fleeing to India.[^cite_note-308] The AA also attacked the Danyawaddy Naval Base in Kyaukphyu Township, which is the main naval base of the junta in southern Rakhine, and captured a junta outpost in Mrauk-U Township.[^cite_note-309]

On 10 January, the Arakan Army attacked the Light Infantry Battalion 539 and Artillery Battalion 377 stationed near Kan Sauk village in Kyauktaw Township. It captured the Artillery Battalion 377 base on the Sittwe-Yangon highway on 14 January, and later the Light Infantry Battalion 539 on 16 January after nearly 300 soldiers of the battalion surrendered.[^cite_note-310] On 14 January, the group seized the Paletwa Township and declared it a "Military Council-free area."[^cite_note-311] The [Government of India](553883.md) meanwhile announced a plan to fence the [Indo-Myanmar border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Myanmar_border) in response to over 600 Tatmadaw soldiers fleeing to India since the Arakan Army had resumed its offensive.[^cite_note-312]

On 24 January, the Arakan Army recaptured Pauktaw, concluding a three-month battle.[^cite_note-313] Meanwhile, at least 229 [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) personnel had fled to Bangladesh amidst the resumed fighting.[^cite_note-314] On 5 February, a Bangladeshi woman and a Rohingya man died from a mortar shell that fell on the Ghumdum border in [Bandarban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandarban), reportedly fired by Burmese forces.[^cite_note-DT-315]

The Arakan Army declared that it had defeated Light Infantry Battalions 379 and 541 on 6 February, gaining effective control of Minbya Township.[^cite_note-316] By the following day it captured the Kyauktaw Township,[^cite_note-317] while having seized more than 170 junta bases.[^cite_note-318] On 8 February, the Arakan Army declared that it had taken complete control over the Mrauk-U Township after capturing the base of Police Battalion 31.[^cite_note-mrauku-319][^cite_note-320] In response to the group's successes, the junta blew up bridges in Kyauktaw Township and [Sittwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe).[^cite_note-321]

On 15 February, the Arakan Army captured the Myebon Township.[^cite_note-322] On 16 February, Tatmadaw forces retreated from Ma-ei town of [Taungup Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taungup_Township), allowing the Arakan Army to take control. The soldiers also blew up two bridges while retreating.[^cite_note-323] On 22 February, the group captured the Myoma police station in Ponnagyun Township.[^cite_note-324] It also claimed to have killed around 80 soldiers in [Ramree Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramree_Township) during clashes from 24 to 26 February.[^cite_note-325] On 5 March it captured the Ponnagyun Township.[^cite_note-326] 177 soldiers fled to the Bandarban district of Bangladesh on 12 March.[^cite_note-327] Meanwhile, the group captured the Ramree Township on the same day after a three-month battle.[^cite_note-328] It captured [Rathedaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rathedaung) town on 17 March, as well as the headquarters of Light Infantry Battalions 536, 537 and 538, bringing the number of military bases captured by the group to 180, while around 200 surviving soldiers fled the area.[^cite_note-329] On 20 March, the group claimed that Rohingya conscripts were among the dead Tatmadaw forces after it seized the three bases.[^cite_note-330] Residents of the state, Rohingya activists and the Arakan Army later also stated that the junta was forcing Rohingyas to protest against the group.[^cite_note-331] The Arakan Army on 25 March captured the base of Light Infantry Battalion 552 in Buthidaung Township and Ta Man Thar outpost in Maungdaw Township where about 100 security personnel of the junta surrendered. The group also captured the Taung Bazar military camp in Buthidaung Township during the same day after clashing with the 234th Infantry Battalion, killing eight soldiers.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]

Clashes between the Arakan Army and the junta broke out near the Western Military Command's headquarters of [Ann Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Township) and in the [Ngape Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngape_Township) of [Magway Region](493360.md) on 24 March, after the junta attacked a base of the group. The Arakan Army clashed with Light Infantry Battalion 346 and other junta forces in Lone village of Ann Township on 26 March, claiming to have killed fifteen soldiers and wounding another fifteen while receiving two injuries to its own forces. In addition, it also attacked junta troops in Tone Gyi and Chaung Phyar villages of Ngape Township on 26 and 28 March respectively, claiming to kill ten soldiers and wounding eighteen others. The group also stated that it clashed with the Light Infantry Battalion 563 in Myebon Township.[^cite_note-Padein-3][^cite_note-334] Narinjara News cited residents as stating that soldiers and all family members of personnel of Light Infantry Battalion 372 and its supply unit in Ann Township retreating to the headquarters of Western Military Command on 27 March, while clashes were taking place in Pyaung Thay and Nga Let Kya villages of the township.[^cite_note-335] On 2 April, the Arakan Army stated that it had cut off access to the road connecting Ann Township with the town of Padein in Magway Region.[^cite_note-Padein-3]

Clashes broke out near the Tha Htay hydropower plant in [Thandwe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thandwe_Township) on 22 April.[^cite_note-336] Tatmadaw troops blew up a bridge linking two villages in Buthidaung Township on 25 April, and a major bridge on the Mayu river in the same township on 6 May after losing five military battalion headquarters, including that of the Light Infantry Battalion 552, the Light Infantry Battalion 564 on 5 April, the Light Infantry Battalion 565 on 24 April, Military Operations Command 15 on 2 May and Light Infantry Battalion 551 on 3 May. The group also captured four military bases in Maungdaw Township, including that of Number 1 Border Guard Police Battalion and Number 10 Border Guard Police Battalion.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] The Arakan Army stated on 6 May that around 200 soldiers had surrendered after it captured the Number 15 Operation Command Headquarters in Buthidaung.[^cite_note-339] It declared complete control over Buthidaung Township on 18 May, after seizing the bases of the Strategic Military Command, Light Infantry Battalions 234, 345 and 352, Artillery Battalion 378 and a logistics and engineering battalion. Clashes meanwhile continued outside [Buthidaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung) town.[^cite_note-340]

By early June 2024, only five outposts on the border with Bangladesh remained under the control of the junta, as the Arakan Army captured the Border Guard Station No. 6 in Inn Din village on 6 June.[^cite_note-341] 28 more soldiers of the Tatmadaw fled to Bangladesh and were handed over to the Border Guard Bangladesh on 11 June after escaping from the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-342] Meanwhile, the group claimed to have captured ten junta bases in Maungdaw Township from 1 June to 13 June, including three headquarters of the [Border Guard Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Forces) and an army base, killing over 200 personnel.[^cite_note-343]

By 25 June, the Arakan Army had captured the town of [Thandwe](2969521.md), with the junta's security forces retreating to the [Thandwe Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thandwe_Airport).[^cite_note-344] A [World Food Programme](33582.md) warehouse was meanwhile robbed and burnt in Maungdaw Township on 23 June. The Arakan Army blamed the attack on the Tatmadaw and local Rohingyas, while [Nay San Lwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nay_San_Lwin) of the Free Rohingya Coalition advocacy group blamed both the Tatmadaw and the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-345]

Around sixty junta personnel attempting to flee to Bangladesh from the Arakan Army were forced to return on 14 July by the Bangladeshi authorities.[^cite_note-346] Tatmadaw personnel retreating from Thandwe Township to [Gwa Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwa_Township) blew up a bridge near the town of [Kyeintali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyeintali) on 24 July.[^cite_note-347] On 29 July, the Arakan Army began attacking the headquarters of the 5th Border Guard Police Battalion, the last junta outpost in Maungdaw. The residents stated that the Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army, Rohingya Solidarity Organization, and forcibly recruited Rohingya conscripts were fighting alongside the junta personnel.[^cite_note-348]
