The Arakan Army began assaulting the town of [Kyeintali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyeintali) and its countryside on 10 August, taking complete control on 14 August. The forces of the junta regrouped between Kyeintali and the town of [Gwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwa,_Myanmar).[^cite_note-Kyeintali-349] The group announced the capture of Myanmar Navy's Maung Shwe Lay Navy Base on 7 September, marking the completion of its offensive of bringing the Thandwe Township under its control, killing over 400 security personnel of the junta in the battle.[^cite_note-350] 123 members of the Tatmadaw and Border Guard Police seeking refuge in Bangladesh were deported back to Myanmar on 29 September.[^cite_note-351] Meanwhile, the Arakan Army began a siege of the Mae Taung strategic bases in Ann Township on 26 September, seizing all of them by 7 October. In addition, all the remaining troops in the area of Kyeintali retreated to Gwa.[^cite_note-MaeTaung-352]

By November, the Arakan Army had encircled the Western Command of the Tatmadaw as well as the town of Ann, after having captured the bases of Sway Chaung and Mae Taung outposts, Light Infantry Battalions 372 and 373, a field medical battalion, a logistics battalion, a security affairs battalion, as well as a military hospital in the Ann Township. It also seized the Mount Pan Mrauk artillery battalion base in the Taungup Township. On 6 November, the group seized the bases of Light Infantry Battalion 371 and an Ordnance Corps.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354] Anti-junta groups Chin Defense Force (Asho) and [People's Revolution Alliance (Magway)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Revolution_Alliance_\(Magway\)) meanwhile started clashing with the Tatmadaw on the Ann-Padan Road along the border between Rakhine and [Magwe Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magwe_Region), in order to prevent the junta troops from reinforcing their compatriots.[^cite_note-355] On 28 November, the Arakan Army declared that they had completely captured the town of [Taungup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taungup) and ten military camps after clashing with the junta for 17 days.[^cite_note-356]

On 8 December, the Arakan Army seized complete control of the [Maungdaw Township](15160625.md) and the [Bangladesh–Myanmar border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93Myanmar_border) after capturing Border Guard Police Battalion No. 5, the last stronghold of the junta along the border after a long 6 months [Battle of Maungdaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Maungdaw). The group also captured Brigadier General [Thurein Tun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thurein_Tun), head of Military Operation Command 15, along with other soldiers, including around 80 Rohingyas.[^cite_note-357] The group also captured the Taungup Township on 14 December after taking control of the headquarters of the Military Operations Command 5.[^cite_note-358] The group captured the Western Military Command in Ann town on 20 December after two weeks of clashes, with many junta troops surrendering and being evacuated.[^cite_note-359]

On 29 December, the Arakan Army declared that it had captured the town of [Gwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwa,_Myanmar) after seizing all the military bases there, including those of Light Infantry Battalions 562 and 563. It however continued clashing with the junta which launched a counterattack after withdrawing from the town.[^cite_note-360] The group stated that around 700 junta troops and several military commanders were killed defending the town, including Colonel Than Soe Win, commander of the 11th Light Infantry Division. Having seized complete control of Gwa Township, which marked its seizure of 14 out of 17 townships in the Rakhine State, the Arakan Army stated that it was ready to implement a ceasefire as long as the junta was willing to resolve the conflict through political means.[^cite_note-361]

### 2025

On 1 January 2025, the Arakan Army attacked a junta checkpoint in the Taung Pone Gyi village near [Pandaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandaung_Township) of [Bago Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bago_Region) and located on the border between Rakhine and Bago. The checkpoint served as a defense for nearby arms factories in the Bago Region.[^cite_note-362] By late-January 2025, the group claimed to have captured multiple military bases from the junta along the borders of Rakhine State, Magway, Bago, and [Ayeyarwady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayeyarwady_Region) regions. Among the seized positions were the Moe Hti base located between Taungup and Padaung townships, as well as the Point 263 and Point 369 bases along the Rakhine-Ayeyarwady border.[^cite_note-Jan2025-4]

The Arakan Army also clashed with the junta near the [Shwe Thaung Yan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shwethaungyan_Subtownship) town in Ayeyarwady Region in late-January 2025, with residents reporting that it had captured two villages. In addition, the group reported that junta soldiers deserted the Goksiyoe village on the Rakhine-Magwe border and the Arakan Army was advancing along the Ann-Padan road towards Magwe Region's [Ngape Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngape_Township) with assistance from three local resistance groups. Chin Defence Force (Asho) stated that it captured 17 junta soldiers as they were retreating from Ann Township along the Ann-Padan road.[^cite_note-Jan2025-4]

The NGO Fortify Rights on 23 February alleged that the Arakan Army was executing civilians and captured soldiers, while also urging the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) to investigate after a video emerged showing fighters of the group and other men abusing and executing two captured junta soldiers. The Arakan Army admitted that the two soldiers were executed by its members on 7 February 2024 in Kyauktaw Township, and was caused by them desiring revenge on the junta soldiers for their war crimes.[^cite_note-FortifyRights-363]

The Arakan Army on 7 February 2025 began shelling the junta's positions in Sittwe in response to its artillery attacks, which it had been carrying out since December 2024 on areas held by the group.[^cite_note-364] The group relaunched its assault on the Danyawaddy naval base in the Kyaukphyu Township on 20 February, attacking the surrounding military outposts and a police battalion base.[^cite_note-365] On 24 February, it was reported to have encircled all bases of the junta, which was accused of blocking the evacuation of residents from the Kyakhphyu Township.[^cite_note-366] On 27 February, heavy clashes broke out between the Arakan Army and the junta in the [Thabaung Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thabaung_Township) of Ayeyarwady Region.[^cite_note-367]

By mid-March, the Arakan Army had advanced towards Sittwe, and was attacking the Min Gan naval base of the junta north of the city as well as Thone Soung village. In response, the junta reinforced the base and areas around Byaing Phyu village.[^cite_note-368] The Arakan Army and its allies also begun attacking the Nayungyo junta base in Pandaung Township which defended a group of ordnance factories in Bago Region, capturing most of the ouposts surrounding the base.[^cite_note-369] In late-March, the Arakan Army intensified its attacks on the Ayeyarwady Region, sparking clashes in [Lemyethna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lemyethna_Township), [Thabaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thabaung_Township) and [Yegyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yegyi_Township) townships.[^cite_note-370]

The Three Brotherhood Alliance declared a unilateral ceasefire for a month on 1 April following the [2025 Myanmar earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Myanmar_earthquake), in order to allow relief and rescue operations.[^cite_note-371] The junta also announced a ceasefire against opposition groups on the following day.[^cite_note-372] Some fighting however continued while the Arakan Army captured the Nyaungyo strategic base in Bago Region on the same day.[^cite_note-373] The junta continued bombing townships despite the ceasefire, carrying out 409 attacks on townships held by the Arakan Army between 2 and 22 April, according to the ULA.[^cite_note-374]

Both the Three Brotherhood Alliance and the Myanmar junta later extended the ceasefire for a month in May 2025.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] The United League of Arakan banned men aged between 18 and 45, and women between 18 and 25, from leaving Rakhine State in late-May 2025 as the Arakan Army was conscripting residents of the state.[^cite_note-377] On 26 May, the Arakan Army killed Brigadier General Kyaw Myo Aung, a strategist of the 11th Infantry Division, alongside an army captain near Kyaukphyu. The group also captured several junta positions defending a police battalion headquarters in the Pyaing Si Kay village of Kyaukphyu Township.[^cite_note-KyawMyoAung-378]

Both the Myanmar junta and the Three Brotherhood Alliance later extended the ceasefire till the end of June.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] The Arakan Army in early June however began besieging the air defense base on the Nat Yay Kan Mountain, the last position still in the hands of the junta on the Ann-Padan road,[^cite_note-381] and also continued its assault on the Kyaukphyu Township, targeting outposts defending Police Battalion 32. More than 40,000 civilians were displaced in Kyaukphyu Township due to the battle.[^cite_note-382]

After the expiration of the ceasefire, the conflict escalated in Kyaukphyu Township in July 2025,[^cite_note-383] with the Arakan Army taking control of the road connecting Kyaukphyu town to Danyawaddy naval base by mid-July.[^cite_note-384] Clashes re-erupted in the [Ngathaingchaung Subtownship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngathaingchaung_Subtownship) of the Ayeyarwady Region in early-August 2025, after being paused since June, in addition to clashes breaking out near Set Set Yo Village and [Lemyethna Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lemyethna_Township).[^cite_note-385] The Myanmar junta later launched a nationwide counteroffensive ahead of the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Myanmar_general_election) scheduled later in the year, with junta forces trying to retake lost areas in Ayeyarwady Region and trying to advance into Rakhine along the Ann-Padan road. The Arakan Army however managed to repel their attack.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387]

The Arakan Army on 28 September captured the Gokkyi outpost, a critical supply base for the junta in the Magwe Region.[^cite_note-388] In October 2025, the junta began fortifying Sittwe and areas along the road to Ponnagyun Township, causing the Arakan Army to pull back slightly in the township.[^cite_note-389] On 10 November, the junta troops recaptured the Thaing Chaung Taung base in Kyaukphyu Township. Around 30 soldiers were killed on 15 November after an ambush by the Arakan Army in the Minpyin village.[^cite_note-390]

### 2026

Following the completion of the process of the general election in the townships held by the junta in December 2025, the Arakan Army resumed its advance on Sittwe and Kyaukphyu in early January 2026.[^cite_note-391] On 4 January, Colonel Han Lin Aung, chief of Military Operations Command No. 10, was killed alongside around ten soldiers during an ambush by the Arakan Army on a column of troops in Padaung Township.[^cite_note-392]

The Arakan Army advanced to the outskirts of Sittwe by late-February 2026, clashing with the junta around Kyay Taw village as well as near its Regional Operations Command and the Shwe Min Gan naval base.[^cite_note-393] By late-April 2026, the junta had increased the deployment of naval vessels in waters near southern Rakhine and patrols of [UAVs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UAVs) over southern townships controlled by the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-394] The retaking of the Kangyi village in Magwe Region from local resistance groups by the junta cut off an important trade route to Rakhine, further tightening the blockade of the state.[^cite_note-395]

By early June, the Arakan Army had surrounded the Tatmadaw's naval bases in Sittwe and Kyuakphyu. Intense fighting was reported between the AA and the Tatmadaw's naval vessels positioned in the Bay of Bengal and along the Kaladan River southeast of Sittwe. Between May 29 and June 1, more than 40 junta soldiers were killed, including a captain. As a result, military troops retreated closer to the Taung Maw Oo naval base, which is supported by troops from 11th Light Infantry Division, and the Danyawaddy Naval Headquarters.[^cite_note-396]

As the Arakan Army advanced further, the military regime escalated its airstrikes on civilian areas in July 2026.[^cite_note-Jul2026-397] The town of [Thandwe](2969521.md) was impacted the most [^cite_note-398] According to data compiled by *[The Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irrawaddy)*, a total of 53 civilians were killed and 163 wounded over the months of July and August.[^cite_note-399]

In September 2026, the junta launched Operation 4926 to recapture territory from the Arakan Army, launching attacks on the positions of the group around Ponnagyun-Sittwe border and the Kyaukphyu-Ramree border. The offensive however began faltering as regime troops began taking heavy losses and withdrawing.[^cite_note-400]

## Humanitarian crisis



[Kutupalong refugee camp](54548485.md) in [Bangladesh](3454.md)

4,000 non-Muslim villagers were evacuated by security forces in late-August amid ongoing clashes. According to estimates by [Rohingya refugees living in Bangladesh](53544313.md), around 2,000 people crossed into Bangladesh.[^cite_note-401] The number of displaced non-Muslims later rose to 30,000 in early-September.[^cite_note-402] According to the United Nations, at least 270,000 Rohingya fled to Bangladesh in just two weeks following renewed violence that began on 25 August 2017.[^cite_note-Exodus-403][^cite_note-Ben-36] Prior to which, two refugee camps in [Cox's Bazar](2271204.md) in Bangladesh were already home to nearly 34,000 Rohingya refugees.[^cite_note-NYT-404] Some 23,000 others remain internally displaced within Rakhine State.[^cite_note-reliefweb-405] By late October 2017, around 200 had drowned while trying to cross into Bangladesh on boats since late-August.[^cite_note-406]

In February 2017, the [government of Bangladesh](18420484.md) announced that it planned to relocate the new refugees and another 232,000 Rohingya refugees already in the country to [Bhasan Char](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thengar_Char), a sedimentary island in the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md).[^cite_note-bangladeshbbc-407][^cite_note-bangladeshaj-408] The island first appeared around 2007, formed from washed down [silt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silt) from the [Meghna River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meghna_River).[^cite_note-bangladeshbbc-407][^cite_note-bangladeshaj-408] The nearest inhabited land, [Hatiya Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatiya_Island) is around 30 kilometres (19 mi) away.[^cite_note-bangladeshbbc-407] News agencies quoted a regional official describing the plan as "terrible".[^cite_note-bangladeshaj-408] The move has received substantial opposition from a number of quarters. Human rights groups have described the plan as a forced relocation.[^cite_note-bangladeshaj-408][^cite_note-bangladeshbbc-407]



Displaced [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Myanmar) families at a makeshift camp in [Maungdaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maungdaw).

Hundreds of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Myanmar) who complained of violence in their villages have also fled to Bangladesh.[^cite_note-join-409][^cite_note-BdNews-410][^cite_note-DailyStar-411] At least 3,000 of an estimated 8,000 Hindus living in [Maungdaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maungdaw), [Buthidaung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buthidaung) and [Sittwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe) have fled since the start of military operations, some of whom were internally displaced, whilst others fled to Bangladesh. More than 500 Hindus returned to Maungdaw from Sittwe in December 2017.[^cite_note-412]

From 25 August 2017, when the [Tatmadaw](20394.md) (Myanmar Armed Forces) launched a [military operation](666301.md) in response to attacks by Rohingya insurgents, to 22 September 2017, satellite images showed that Rohingya villages were still being burned and an estimated 429,000 refugees had fled into [Bangladesh](3454.md)[^cite_note-413] (creating a larger weekly outflow of refugees than the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md)), out of a pre-violence Rohingya population of about 1 million in Rakhine state.[^cite_note-eco1-125]

On 5 October 2017, the Bangladeshi [Minister of Disaster Management and Relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Disaster_Management_and_Relief) [Mofazzal Hossain Chowdhury Maya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mofazzal_Hossain_Chowdhury) announced that the world's largest refugee camp would be built to house Rohingya refugees. Rohingyas settled across the border in 23 camps were to be transferred to one central camp in [Cox's Bazar](452471.md), with all the other camps being closed after the completion of the transfer.[^cite_note-414] By the end of October 2017, the UN estimated that over 600,000 refugees had fled to Bangladesh since armed clashes resumed two months earlier.[^cite_note-CBS_News-415][^cite_note-Ottawa-416][^cite_note-veconomist-417] Bangladesh approved a plan on 28 November 2017 to temporarily shelter 100,000 Rohingya on the island.[^cite_note-418]

Around 13,000 Rohingyas fled Myanmar from January to September 2018 according to the [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md) (UNHCR).[^cite_note-419] In 2018 Myanmar began closing IDP camps for Rohingyas and constructing permanent buildings of refuge in their place. The Rohingyas' movement, however, remained restricted. Aid workers and IDPs stated that even those with national verification cards had their movement restricted.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421] Bangladesh's [Minister for Disaster Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Disaster_Management_and_Relief) [Md. Enamur Rahaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Md._Enamur_Rahaman) said on 26 February 2020 that the country had shelved plans to move the Rohingyas to Bhashan Char.[^cite_note-422] Afzalul Haque, director of [Bangladesh Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Navy)'s intelligence unit, told BenarNews in May that all Rohingyas arriving on boats from now on will be moved to the island.[^cite_note-423] Bangladesh began transferring Rohingyas from Cox's Bazar to Bhashan Char on 4 December, with the first batch of 1,600 refugees being transported by boat. Aid organisations and refugees have accused the Bangladeshi government of forcing some of the refugees to go.[^cite_note-424]

About 5,000 civilians in Rakhine State were forced to flee fighting between the Arakan Army and Tatmadaw from early December 2018 to mid-January 2019, per the United Nations.[^cite_note-13rebels-425] This number had swelled to more than 20,000 by the start of April 2019.[^cite_note-Twentyk-426] About 10,000 were displaced from Minbya Township alone from late July to late August.[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] According to statistics collected by a local NGO, the Rakhine Ethnic Congress, the number of IDPs displaced by fighting between the Tatmadaw and Arakan Army had reached 160,000 by January 2020.[^cite_note-429] It was estimated that 40,000 people had been internally displaced in the last week of June amid reignited clashes.[^cite_note-430] The [OCHA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCHA) estimated that 86,383 had been displaced as of 5 August.[^cite_note-431]

The Rakhine Ethnic Congress stated that the number of internally displaced people due to the fighting between the Tatmadaw and Arakan Army reached 234,915 by early November 2020, when the ceasefire between both sides took effect.[^cite_note-432] More than 78,000 people fled resumed fighting between both sides in Rakhine and Chin states since March 2022 according to the [UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Office_for_the_Coordination_of_Humanitarian_Affairs) (UNOCHA).[^cite_note-433]

The UNHCR stated on 4 March 2024 that 350,900 people had been displaced amidst resumed clashes between the Arakan Army and the Tatmadaw since November 2023.[^cite_note-434] In May 2024, it estimated that 45,000 Rohingyas had fled towards Bangladesh due to clashes in Buthidaung and Maungdaw Townships.[^cite_note-MayRohingya-435] An estimated 60,000 Rohingyas fled to Bangladesh in November and December 2024 amidst the intense clashes between the Myanmar junta and the Arakan Army according to [Md. Touhid Hossain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Md._Touhid_Hossain), Adviser for Foreign Affairs of Bangladesh.[^cite_note-436]

### Repatriation



Rohingya refugees in [Cox's Bazar](2271204.md), Bangladesh, protesting against what they view as a dangerous [repatriation](1481067.md) to Myanmar.

[Bangladeshi Foreign Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Bangladesh\)) [Abul Hassan Mahmud Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abul_Hassan_Mahmud_Ali) met with Myanmar officials on 2 October 2017, later stating after their meeting that both countries had agreed on a "joint working group" for the [repatriation](1481067.md) of Rohingya refugees who had fled to Bangladesh.[^cite_note-437]

The governments of Myanmar and Bangladesh signed a [memorandum of understanding](1018279.md) on 23 November 2017 regarding the repatriation of Rohingya refugees to Rakhine State.[^cite_note-438] Bangladesh's Foreign Minister stated that a joint working group composed of UNHCR and members of both nations was to be established within three weeks to fix the final terms for the beginning of the process. He also stated that those returning would be kept in temporary camps near their abandoned homes. Under the deal, Myanmar would ensure that they are not kept in the camps for long and are issued identity cards.[^cite_note-439] The foreign secretaries of both nations met on 19 December to finalise the agreement. Bangladesh's foreign ministry issued a statement saying that the group would "ensure commencement of repatriation within two months" by developing a timetable for verification of identities and logistics.[^cite_note-440]

[Bangladesh](3454.md)'s [foreign ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Bangladesh\)) announced on 15 January 2018 that their government and Myanmar's had bilaterally agreed on a [repatriation](1481067.md) deal for Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh, which would aim to complete the process of repatriation within two years.[^cite_note-441][^cite_note-442] [Win Myat Aye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Win_Myat_Aye), Myanmar's [Minister for Social Welfare, Relief and Resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_Welfare,_Relief_and_Resettlement), also announced that his country would begin repatriating Rohingya refugees beginning on 23 January 2018.[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444] Originally, the government of Myanmar agreed to [repatriate](1481067.md) only 374 Rohingya refugees out of a list of over 8,000 submitted by their Bangladeshi counterparts on 14 March 2018, citing incomplete paperwork as the reason for the slow process,[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446] but four days later they announced that a total of 1,100 "verified" Rohingyas from the list would be repatriated.[^cite_note-447]

On 6 June 2018, the [United Nations](31769.md) and the government of Myanmar signed a memorandum of understanding regarding the repatriation of Rohingya refugees,[^cite_note-448] the details of which were kept secret until they were leaked online on 29 June 2018.[^cite_note-449] The agreement was immediately criticised and rejected by Rohingya leaders, who say it does not address the concerns of their community.[^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451] In a September 2018 interview with *Reuters*, Bangladeshi Prime Minister [Sheikh Hasina](623439.md) accused Myanmar of fabricating excuses to avoid repatriating the Rohingya. She also said the Rohingya would not be allowed to stay permanently in Bangladesh.[^cite_note-452] The two countries agreed to begin repatriation from mid-November.[^cite_note-453] However, most Rohingyas believed it was not safe to return and refused to be repatriated, demanding accountability for abuses against them and guarantees of citizenship.[^cite_note-454] Meanwhile, 624 Rohingyas had returned to Myanmar independently by February 2020.[^cite_note-455]

Bangladesh's foreign secretary [Shahidul Haque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahidul_Haque) told the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) on 28 February 2019 that no Rohingya refugee had agreed to return to Myanmar due to the non-guarantee of a safe and conducive environment. He also said that Bangladesh could not take in any more refugees.[^cite_note-456] After the coup in Myanmar in February 2021, [Chairman of the State Administration Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_State_Administration_Council) [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md) said that the repatriation would continue. Later in May 2021, however, he stated that the country would not take Rohingyas back if their repatriation did not meet the legal requirements, and termed them as foreigners.[^cite_note-457] The [National Unity Government of Myanmar](67413816.md), which was formed in opposition to the State Administration Council, promised to repatriate and grant citizenship to the Rohingyas if it came to power.[^cite_note-458]

## Human rights violations

Main article: [Rohingya genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_genocide)

A 20-page report by United Nations' investigators stated Tatmadaw was responsible for a genocidal campaign against the Rohingya which included mass-murder, sex slavery, kidnappings, deportations and gang rapes. It also blamed the civilian government for helping the military by destroying documents implicating the Tatmadaw, allowing hate speech and not protecting civilians from human rights abuses. The generals named by the report for orchestrating the genocide included Myanmar's commander-in-chief [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md), deputy Commander-in-Chief [Soe Win](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soe_Win_\(general\)), Lt. Gen. Aung Kyaw Zaw, Major General [Maung Maung Soe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maung_Maung_Soe_\(general\)), and Brigadier-General Than Oo.[^cite_note-459] It also accused ARSA of committing human right abuses.[^cite_note-460] The UN stated in April 2019 that both the military and the Arakan Army were committing abuses upon civilians since the start of their conflict.[^cite_note-Twentyk-426]

Myanmar appointed a panel to investigate the alleged abuses in July 2018. It consisted of two non-Burmese members – [Rosario Manalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosario_Manalo), [Kenzo Oshima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenzo_Oshima), as well as two Burmese members – [Aung Tun Thet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aung_Tun_Thet) and the lawyer Mya Thein.[^cite_note-461] It submitted a report to the government on 20 January 2020 and stated that though war crimes as well as abuses had taken place, there was no evidence that the military tried to commit a genocide of the Rohingyas.[^cite_note-462] In addition, it stated that nearly 1,000 people were killed during the clashes and the Tatmadaw committed massacres in four villages.[^cite_note-463]

The [National Unity Government of Myanmar](67413816.md), formed in opposition to the [State Administration Council](66589972.md) military junta which seized power during the [2021 Myanmar coup d'état](66567997.md), recognized that the military had been involved in mass killings and sexual violence against the Rohingya on 24 August 2021, calling for justice to be delivered to victims.[^cite_note-464]

The Arakan Army has also been accused by the Human Rights Watch of perpetrating severe human rights abuses on the Rohingya akin to that of the Tatmadaw, including restrictions on movement, robbery, arbitrary detention, mistreatment, forced labour and forced recruitment.[^cite_note-465]

### Killings of civilians

The Burmese government and Rohingya advocates have both accused the other side of killing civilians. Advocates for the Rohingyas have claimed that many civilians have died in military attacks on villages. The government has accused ARSA of killing civilians, including Hindus and Muslims, some of whom were suspected by ARSA of being government informants.[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-Xinhua-467][^cite_note-Kyodo-468] ARSA in a statement has dismissed government allegations against it as baseless, seeking to present its cause as a defence of Rohingya rights.[^cite_note-469]

Bangladesh's Foreign Minister Abul Hassan Mahmud Ali has stated that Myanmar's security forces have killed over 3,000 Rohingyas during the 2017 clashes.[^cite_note-Peninsula-470] Myanmar officials meanwhile have stated that 163 people have been killed and 91 have gone missing in ARSA attacks since 2016.[^cite_note-471] A [Médecins Sans Frontières](20498.md) survey published in December 2017 reported that 6,700 Rohingyas had been killed by shooting and other violence between 25 August and 24 September. The report was based on interviews in late October and early November with refugees regarding questions on numbers and conditions of deaths of their family members.[^cite_note-MSFsurvey-472]

Some members of the Hindu community have also fled to Bangladesh, with some blaming soldiers and others blaming Buddhist vigilantes for violence against them while others have accused Rohingya insurgents of attacking them on suspicion of being government spies.[^cite_note-BawKya-473] Some of the Hindus who migrated to Bangladesh stated that their villages are also experiencing killings, torture and arson attacks like those of Rohingyas. 86 Hindus were killed by unidentified men in Myanmar according to the refugees and Rana Dasgupta, head of the [Bangladesh Hindu Buddhist Christian Unity Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Hindu_Buddhist_Christian_Unity_Council).[^cite_note-join-409][^cite_note-BdNews-410][^cite_note-DailyStar-411]

According to the Rakhine Ethnic Congress, 342 civilians were killed and 740 injured during the fighting between the Arakan Army and the Tatmadaw from 2018 to 2020. In addition, 33 civilians were killed and 67 wounded due to landmines left behind as a result of the conflict.[^cite_note-474] The [United League of Arakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_League_of_Arakan) accused the Tatmadaw of killing 179 civilians and wounding 468 during clashes with its armed front, the Arakan Army, from 13 November 2023 to 21 March 2024.[^cite_note-ULA-475]

#### Accusations against Arakan Army

The Arakan Army was accused of killing civilians and beheading Rohingyas after capturing the town of Buthidaung in May 2024.[^cite_note-MayRohingya-435] The group later rejected the accusations as baseless.[^cite_note-476] At least 150 Rohingyas were killed on 5 August 2024 due to shelling and drone attacks while trying to flee towards Bangladesh due to clashes in the town of Maungdaw. The survivors, as well as Rohingya activists and the Myanmar government blamed the Arakan Army, which denied the accusations.[^cite_note-477]

[Nay San Lwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nay_San_Lwin), co-founder of Free Rohingya Coalition, in January 2025 alleged that the Arakan Army had killed at least 2,500 Rohingyas and forced around 40,000 to flee Myanmar between March and August 2024.[^cite_note-478] The NGO Fortify Rights also accused the group of killing civilians and captured junta soldiers later in the same month.[^cite_note-FortifyRights-363]

The Arakan Rohingya National Council on 3 August accused the Arakan Army of massacring over 600 people in Htan Shauk Kham village of Buthidaung Township. The group however denied the claim.[^cite_note-479]

#### Accusations against Myanmar's security forces

The [Border Guards Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guards_Bangladesh) station chief of the Ghumdum border post in Bangladesh accused Myanmar's military of firing on fleeing Rohingyas on 26 August 2017. An [Agence France-Presse](471981.md) reporter counted more than a dozen mortar shells and several heavy machine gun rounds fired by Burmese security forces on fleeing Rohingyas.[^cite_note-480]

Reports of mass killings of Rohingyas by the military and Buddhist vigilantes in Chut Pyin village near Rathedaung emerged in September 2017. Chris Lewa stated that they had received reports of 130 people being killed in the village. A video provided to [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_\(Australia\)) by a human rights monitor purportedly showed the village burning and in another clip of freshly dug earth mound, allegedly graves of those killed.[^cite_note-481]

In interviews with [The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian) in the same month, Rohingya villagers of Tula Toli accused Myanmar's armed forces of sweeping through the village and murdering scores of people.[^cite_note-482] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) in December 2017 accused the military of killing several hundred villagers of Tula Toli and burning the bodies of victims in pits to destroy evidence. The report was partly based on interviews with 18 people. HRW also accessed images detailing complete destruction of Tula Toli and nearby Dual Toli while neighbouring non-Muslim villages were unharmed.[^cite_note-483]

In interviews with [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) in October 2017, 14 villagers of Muang Nu and surrounding villages in the Chin Tha Mar village area blamed Myanmar's army of beating, sexually assaulting and killing Rohingya villagers seeking safety in a residential compound on 27 August. Satellite imagery accessed by the group showed near total destruction of Muang Nu and Hpaung Taw Pyin village. Though the group was not able to verify the death toll, some interviewees stated there were 100 or more bodies.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485]

Myanmar's army discovered a mass-grave containing 10 unidentified bodies in [Inn Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din) village, located 50 kilometres (31 mi) north of Rakhine's capital [Sittwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe) on 18 December. The army stated two days later that a senior officer had been appointed to investigate whether security forces were involved in their killing. Myanmar's commander-in-chief [Min Aung Hlaing](38278640.md) stated that a five-member team was involved in the investigation.[^cite_note-486] The attacks on Rohingyas in the mixed village had been documented by Amnesty in August as well as burning of their homes.[^cite_note-bbc.com-487]

The military issued details of the investigation team's findings on 10 January 2018, stating that some of the soldiers were involved in extrajudicial killings of 10 Rohingya, who were among the 200 involved in attacking Buddhist villagers and security forces in Inn Din. The statement added that the ten who were arrested, were killed instead of being handed over to the police. It added that action would be taken against ethnic Rakhine villagers and soldiers involved. AFP meanwhile reported that Rohingya refugees from the village claimed that those killed were civilians and not terrorists.[^cite_note-488] ARSA on 13 January denied that the Rohingyas who were killed were associated with them, claiming they were innocent civilians.[^cite_note-489] Seven soldiers involved in the killings were sentenced to 10-year prison terms with [hard labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_labour).[^cite_note-NaingAung-490][^cite_note-491]

The [Associated Press](18935732.md) reported on 1 February 2018 that five mass graves of Rohingya civilians had been found around the village of [Gu Dar Pyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gu_Dar_Pyin), and based on the testimonies of survivors and time-stamped cellphone videos, the report claimed they were victims of the [Gu Dar Pyin massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gu_Dar_Pyin_massacre). However, the government of Myanmar denied the report, stating only "terrorists" were killed in self-defense and "carefully buried" by soldiers.[^cite_note-APgraves-492][^cite_note-493]

On 22 March 2019, seven civilians were killed in shelling on Buthidaung township's Si Taung Gyi village blamed on the Tatmadaw by the villagers as well as the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-494] Six Rohingya Muslims who were collecting bamboo, were killed and 13 injured upon being fired by a helicopter on 3 April in a village of Bathidaung township according to a local resident and Maungdaw Township's MP. The Tatmadaw, however, claimed that they were affiliates of the Arakan Army whom they were hunting down after being notified about their presence in the area, while nine insurgents were injured.[^cite_note-495] Two Rakhine villagers from Pyi Taw Thar of [Minbya Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minbya_Township) were killed on 23 April 2020. A victim's brother claimed they were shot dead by the military without provocation, however, a Tatmadaw spokesman accused them of being Arakan Army insurgents who refused to stop for checking.[^cite_note-496]

Six civilians were shot dead and eight injured in Kyauk Tan village of Rathedaung Township on 2 May at a school where 275 men had been detained over alleged links with Arakan Army. The military claimed that they had tried to attack them, however, this account was disputed by local politicians and witnesses who claimed that the firing was unprovoked.[^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498][^cite_note-499] Firing between Myauk Taung and Marlar Taung villages of [Kyauktaw Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyauktaw_Township) on 19 May in revenge for a mine attack on a military convoy, left a civilian dead according to the villagers. Two others were injured in Marlar Taung village due to shelling.[^cite_note-500] Residents of Minbya Township's Pan Myaung village also accused the army of killing three civilians and injuring four in shelling on 25 August despite there being no insurgents present there.[^cite_note-501]

The official website of Tatmadaw on 31 August announced investigation into the conduct of soldiers at Gu Dar Pyin saying they disregarded instructions.[^cite_note-502] It stated in November that court-martial proceedings had begun against soldiers who were involved in "accidents".[^cite_note-503] In February 2020 it announced opening of investigations into the alleged massacres in Muang Nu and Chut Pyin villages.[^cite_note-504] Three officers were announced on 30 June to have been convicted for their actions at Gu Dar Pyin, though their crimes were not revealed to the public.[^cite_note-505]

In September 2020, video testimony of two former soldiers, who stated that they had deserted, taken by the Arakan Army was published online. The two claimed that they were involved in killing of more than 150 Rohingyas along with their battalions in Buthidaung and Muangdaw Townships in August 2017. The two later fled to Bangladesh in August 2020 and were transferred to [The Hague](30269.md).[^cite_note-506]

Shelling by the army on 2 December killed three civilians and injured six according to residents and an MP of [Mrauk U](2636746.md).[^cite_note-507] Four Rohingya children were killed in Htaikhtoo Pauk village of Buthidaung Township, with five others being injured along with their teacher on 7 January 2020. Both the military and Arakan Army accused each other of planting the mine.[^cite_note-508]

Three civilians were killed and 30 injured in villages of Mrauk U and Kyauktaw townships due to attacks on 13 March blamed on the army by their residents. The villagers stated that the attacks came in reprisal due to an ambush by the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-509] On 1 April, five civilians were killed and seen injured in attacks blamed on the military by some residents.[^cite_note-510]

18 villagers were abducted from the villages of Tin Ma Thit and Tin Ma Gyi in Kyauktaw Township by the Tatmadaw on 16 March, for aiding the Arakan Army, according to eyewitnesses. The body of one of the villagers was found the next day with bullet wounds. The police agreed to take up their case in December 2020, after their families demanded to know what had happened to the others for over nine months.[^cite_note-511]

Eight civilians were killed by shelling on the Kyauk Seik village of [Ponnagyun Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponnagyun_Township) on 13 April, blamed on the military by both the villagers and the Arakan Army.[^cite_note-512] Villagers of Kyauktaw Township accused the Tatmadaw of killing a civilian and injuring 13 others in shooting while targeting rebels on 16 April.[^cite_note-513]

A driver working for the [World Health Organization](33583.md) was killed in a mine attack while a government health worker was injured in an attack on their vehicle on 20 April; the military and Arakan Army blamed each other for the incidents.[^cite_note-514] Four civilians were killed in Paletwa Township on 22 April due to shelling. The Tatmadaw blamed the Arakan Army, while the group blamed the military. Witnesses also reported that the Tatmadaw killed four people in Minbya Township. The military claimed that two of them were Arakan Army members who were shot while trying to flee.[^cite_note-515]

A mine blast killed 2 Rohingya children and wounded another on 13 May, with both the Arakan Army and Tatmadaw accusing each other.[^cite_note-516] Eyewitnesses reported deaths of two villagers and injury to three others in the army's attack on Chein Hkali village of Rathedaung township during the clashes the lasted from 12 to 13 July. Rakhine Ethnic Congress' secretary Zaw Zaw Tun said that over 180 people had been killed since the start of clashes with AA.[^cite_note-517] According to Radio Free Asia in August 2020, 274 had been killed.[^cite_note-518]

Tatmadaw was accused of killing two civilians of Kyauktaw township during 26–27 August. A man was shot dead in Shan Ywa village on 26 August and the next day in Apaukaw village, a woman was killed while two others injured in shelling, according to their relatives.[^cite_note-519] Amnesty International said on 12 October that it had received reports of two men being killed after being detained in Hpa Yar Paung village in Kyauktaw Township on 3 September and images of MM2 landmines, regularly used by Tatmadaw, being planted in civilian areas.[^cite_note-520] Four people were killed and 10 injured due to a shell landing in Nyaung Khet Kan village of Myebon Township on 8 September. [Pyithu Hluttaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyithu_Hluttaw) lawmaker Pe Than accused Tatmadaw of being behind the attack.[^cite_note-521]

Residents of Pyin Shae village of Buthidaung Township accused the Tatmadaw of recruiting 15 villagers, including children, to guide them through an area mined by the Arakan Army on 5 October. Two Rohingya children were killed after the insurgents ambushed the group according to the villagers. The Tatmadaw denied any involvement and blamed the Arakan Army, which in turn claimed that there was no clash in the village and that the military must have killed them. In Minbya Township, three Rohingya men were later killed by the Tatmadaw. The military claimed that they were violating the nighttime curfew and had not stopped their boat when asked to, though the residents stated that the shooting occurred around 10 a.m.[^cite_note-522]

A boat hired by the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) was struck by the [Myanmar Navy](9502915.md) while travelling on the [Mayu river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayu_\(river\)) between Pyin Win and Kan Pyin villages of Rathedaung Township on 28 October, killing one person and injuring two others per witnesses.[^cite_note-523]

Two children were killed by the Tatmadaw on 2 August 2022 when it fired on the boat transporting them across the [Kaladan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaladan_River) to Toe Ma Wa village in Paletwa Township.[^cite_note-boat-278] On 28 August, three civilians were killed due to shelling by the Tatmadaw hitting Kin Seik village in Mrauk-U Township.[^cite_note-Aug2022-284] Four civilians were killed from shelling in Minbya and Kyauktaw townships on 17 October.[^cite_note-524] Three children were killed in [Hakha Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakha_Township) in Chin state and Rathedaung Township in Rakhine on 19 October due to shelling by the military.[^cite_note-525] A family of three was killed when the Tatmadaw shelled Pisi village in Kyauktaw Township on 31 October.[^cite_note-526]

Eight civilians were killed in Sin Ingyi village of Ponnagyun Township by the Tatmadaw on 10 November according to the residents, who also stated that the troops set some houses on fire.[^cite_note-527] On 16 November, at least 17 civilians were killed due to the Tatmadaw shelling the Jeitchaung village in Maungdaw Township and the Chaungtu village in Kyauktaw Township.[^cite_note-Diplomat-295][^cite_note-528]

Four civilians were killed in Mrauk U and Kyauktaw townships on 13 November 2023 and ten were wounded by the Tatmadaw according to other villagers,[^cite_note-529] while ten were killed by it after it attacked Pauktaw Township on 16 November.[^cite_note-530] Shelling by the Tatmadaw killed three civilians and wounded six in Mrauk-U Township on 24 December according to the [Three Brotherhood Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Brotherhood_Alliance).[^cite_note-531] The township's residents accused the junta of killing six civilians sheltering in the Hpayar Paw IDP camp on 28 December.[^cite_note-police-307]

The Arakan Army accused the Light Infantry Battalion 540 of killing four civilian in Mrauk-U Township on 9 January 2024 after clashes with the group. Residents of Apauk Wa village in Kyauktaw Township also accused the Light Infantry Battalion 539 of killing two residents.[^cite_note-532] Six civilians were killed by shelling and airstrikes in Thayet Tapin and Weygyi Dunk villages of Kyauktaw Township according to the Arakan Army,[^cite_note-533] while Lint Zin Ywar Thit village of the same township accused the junta of killing six civilians by shelling.[^cite_note-534] Junta soldiers and policemen held captive by the Arakan Army stated that they had executed nine civilians in Mrauk-U and Minbya townships in January 2024.[^cite_note-535] Shelling by the Tatmadaw on a market in Sittwe left twelve people dead and eighteen wounded on 29 February.[^cite_note-536]

23 Rohingyas were killed and 30 were wounded on 15 March in an airstrike on Thar Dar village in Minbya Township according to residents.[^cite_note-537] Five civilians were killed and thirteen wounded due to airstrikes on Lay Hnin Taung village in Mrauk-U Township on 22 March according to residents.[^cite_note-538] Residents accused the junta of killing nine civilians in Lin Thi village of Thandwe Township, while also killing four civilians and wounding seven others in Kin Chaung village of Maungdaw Township on 14 May. More than ten civilians were killed in Yeazin village of Thandwe Township by the junta on the same day. The Arakan Army accused it of killing civilians and wounding around twenty in Wae Gyi Dauk village of Kyauktaw Township on the foowing day. Nine civilians were killed in Nat Taung Maw village of Thandwe Township on 18 May according to residents.[^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540]

The Arakan Army accused the junta forces of massacring at least 76 civilians in Byine Phyu village of [Sittwe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sittwe_Township) on 30 May 2024. Zaw Min Tun stated that the Tatmadaw killed three people who tried to snatch a gun from an officer, but denied that there had been any mass killings.[^cite_note-ByinePhyu-541] Locals and the National Unity Government meanwhile alleged that the Tatmadaw killed at least 50 civilians. The residents also alleged that those bearing tattoos of the Arakan Army had the skin cut and burnt by the troops, while also forcing some detained men in the village to drink their urine.[^cite_note-542] The Tatmadaw also began attacking the Singaung village in Thandwe Township on 4 June over links to the Arakan Army, killing around ten civilians and injuring as much as 30 others.[^cite_note-543] Six civilians were killed due to attacks by the Tatmadaw on villages of Taungup Township from 15 to 18 June.[^cite_note-544]

Some employees of the [General Administration Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Administration_Department) were killed by the junta troops in mid-August 2024 while fleeing the Arakan's Army assault on the town of [Kyeintali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyeintali).[^cite_note-Kyeintali-349] An airstrike carried out by the [Myanmar Air Force](9484818.md) struck a church in Kyeintali on 15 August killed eleven civilians and wounded eleven others.[^cite_note-545] On 8 September, airstrikes by the Tatmadaw on an Arakan Army medical center for captured junta troops and other inmates in the Shin village of Pauktaw Township, killing 17 people. Airtrikes by the Tatmadaw on Border Guard Police Battalion No. 2 base near Maungdaw on the following day killed over 50 people, including captured junta troops.[^cite_note-546] At least seventeen people were killed in Tan Hlwe Ywar Ma town of Taungup Township due to airstrikes carried out by the Myanmar Air Force on 8 October.[^cite_note-547] The United League of Arakan in mid-November 2024 accused the junta of having killed 735 Rakhine civilians and wounded 1,569 since the beginning of the year.[^cite_note-548]

At least 40 civilians were killed and at least 20 others were wounded due to airstrikes on 8 January 2025 by the Myanmar Air Force on Kyauk Ni Maw village of Ramree Township.[^cite_note-549] On 11 January, around a dozen people were killed in airstrikes on the town of Kyauktaw. 28 women and children were killed and 25 others injured in airstrikes on an Arakan Army detention centre in Mrauk-U Township holding family members of Tatmadaw soldiers on 18 January.[^cite_note-550] An airstrike by the junta on the Tun Yawai Village in Rathedaung Township on 13 May killed 13 civilians and wounded 20 others.[^cite_note-551]

An airstrike by the Tatmadaw on 25 August killed 12 people in Mrauk U.[^cite_note-552] 22 people were killed due to airstrikes in Thayet Thapin village of Kyauktaw Township on 12 September.[^cite_note-553] On 30 October, eight civilians were killed and 22 others wounded in Rakhine state due to attacke by the Tatmadaw, while four civilians were killed in Pan Nilar village of Ponnagyun Township.[^cite_note-554] Three civilians were killed and at least five others were wounded in Taungngu village of Kyaukphyu Township due to bombing by the Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-555]

At least 34 people were killed and many more were injured after the Tatmadaw's [airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Mrauk-U_hospital_airstrike) on a hospital in Mrauk-U on 10 December.[^cite_note-556] On 11 December, bombing by the Tatmadaw on two villages of the Kyaukphyu Township killed eight civilians and wounded ten others according to the Arakan Army. The group also stated that attacks by the junta had killed 1,152 civilians and wounded 2,153 since November 2023.[^cite_note-557]

On 20 January 2026, 21 captured Tatmadaw soldiers and their relatives were killed in airstrikes carried out by the junta on a prison run by the Arakan Army in the Chaung Tu village located between Kyauktaw and Ponnagyun townships.[^cite_note-558] Twenty civilians were killed due to airstrikes on the Yoe Ngu village in Ponnagyun Township on 24 February.[^cite_note-559] Airstrikes on an Arakan Army detention centre in Daletchaung area of Ann Township on 8 March killed 116 prisoners of war and a number of detained civilians, in addition to injuring 32 people.[^cite_note-560] Airstrikes on Kyauktaw by the junta on 17 June killed eight civilians and wounded seventeen others.[^cite_note-561]

On 19 July, five civilians were killed and wounded 26 in airstrikes on Taungup and Thandwe townships.[^cite_note-562] Airstrikes on Thandwe on 24 July resulted in deaths of three civilians and injuries to 21 others.[^cite_note-Jul2026-397] Another fourteen civilians were killed and over a dozen wounded on 10 August due to regime airstrikes.[^cite_note-563]

During interviews conducted by the Arakan Army, two Tatmadaw POWs captured after 16 September stated that superior officers ordered them to kill ethnic Rakhine people and [rustle their livestock](1553326.md).[^cite_note-564] An [airstrike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Kyauktaw_market_airstrike) by the Myanmar military in Kyauktaw on 28 September killed around 50 people and wounded 58 others.[^cite_note-565]

#### Accusations against ARSA

The Myanmar government claimed in a statement that ARSA killed four Muslims, including a village head and a government informant, on 25 August 2017. The next day on 26 August, another Muslim village head and a Hindu child were allegedly killed when ARSA insurgents fired at a monastery. In addition, six Hindus were stated to have been killed when the insurgents attacked a Hindu family.[^cite_note-Kyodo-468] The Office of Myanmar's State Counsellor also blamed ARSA for the killings of five [Daingnets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daingnet_people) on 26 August[^cite_note-566] and seven [Mro people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mro_people_\(Awa_Khami\)) on 31 August.[^cite_note-567]

The mass-graves of 28 Hindus were found by Myanmar's security forces on 24 September 2017 near the village of Ye Baw Kya,[^cite_note-568] with 17 more bodies found on the next day.[^cite_note-569] Three relatives of the deceased said that masked men marched 100 Hindus away from the village before slitting their throats and pushing them into a hole. The relatives recognised some of the attackers as Rohingya Muslims, who told their victims they should not be in possession of official identity cards, which were issued by the government to Hindus but not to Muslims.[^cite_note-Tun-570] After the discovery of the bodies, the Myanmar government claimed the victims were [killed by ARSA insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kha_Maung_Seik_massacre).[^cite_note-Tun-570][^cite_note-571][^cite_note-572] An ARSA spokesman denied the allegation that it was behind the killings and accused Buddhist nationalists of spreading lies to divide Hindus and Muslims.[^cite_note-BawKya-473]

On 9 November 2017, Myint Khyine, the secretary of the Immigration and Population Department, blamed the deaths of 18 village leaders in the past three months in Maungdaw and Buthidaung, on ARSA. The village leaders helped the department to issue national verification cards to Rohingya villagers.[^cite_note-573]

On 22 May 2018, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) released a report based on accounts of witnesses and survivors claiming it had evidence that ARSA rounded up and killed as many as 99 Hindu civilians (53 in Kha Maung Seik and 46 in Ye Bauk Kyar) on 25 August 2017, the same day that ARSA launched a massive attack against Myanmar's security forces. 45 of the victims were found in a mass grave in September 2017.[^cite_note-574][^cite_note-575]

Rohingya refugees and Tom Andrews, a special rapporteur for the UN, have accused ARSA of being behind killings and intimidation of other refugees in the camps in Bangladesh. Bangladesh's authorities have however denied the group's presence.[^cite_note-576][^cite_note-577]

### Arson



A burnt down house in a Rohingya village in northern Rakhine State.



A member of Myanmar's [Border Guard Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Guard_Police) inspects a house burnt down in an arson attack.

ARSA has been accused by the [State Counsellor's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Counsellor_of_Myanmar) office of committing [arson](184695.md) on [Buddhist monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyaung) and local police outposts, killing innocent civilians and planting landmines. ARSA has meanwhile accused the Myanmar Army of using civilians as human shields.[^cite_note-578]

A number of Rohingya properties were set alight during the violence, with residents blaming security forces while the government blamed Rohingya insurgents, suggesting that they had burnt down their own community's property.[^cite_note-579] According to the State Counsellor's office, insurgents had burnt down 88 homes, more than 100 shops, two mosques and eight religious schools, as well as vehicles in a village.[^cite_note-580][^cite_note-Xinhua-467]

[Human Rights Watch](46530.md) in August 2017 said that satellite images showed widespread burning in 10 areas in northern Rakhine. While the causes of the fires could not be determined, the group said that it "compared the locations of these fires with witness statements it has collected and media reports, and found a correlation with some reported incidents where residences have allegedly been deliberately burned."[^cite_note-581]

Chris Lewa, the director of *The Arakan Project*, has accused Myanmar's security forces of burning village after village in a systematic way while also blaming Rohingya arsonists of burning the Buddhist village of Pyu Ma.[^cite_note-582] [Amnesty International](18947898.md)'s crisis response director Tirana Hassan said that evidence points to Myanmar security setting northern Rakhine ablaze. The group also stated that it has credible reports of Rohingya militants burning the homes of ethnic Rakhine and other minorities.[^cite_note-Australian-583]

Human Rights Watch stated on 18 December that 354 villages had been completely or partially destroyed since the clashes that began in August.[^cite_note-BdnewsDec-134]

Amnesty International released a report on 11 March 2018, documenting the bulldozing of intact structures in Rohingya villages and the removal of charred homes by security forces to make way for military bases.[^cite_note-Lewis_2018-145][^cite_note-584]

The Tatmadaw has also been accused of deliberately torching ethnic Rakhine villages during their conflict with Arakan Army. These include Ooyinthar village of Buthidaung, Kyaukmaw Paik Seik village of Myebon and Amyet Taung in Rathedaung, whose destruction was blamed on the army by their residents.[^cite_note-585][^cite_note-586][^cite_note-587] During a press conference held on 30 March 2020, residents of various villages accused the army of deliberately bombing and burning around 150 homes in mid-March.[^cite_note-588]

On 26 May Human Rights Watch called for an unbiased investigation into the burning of 200 buildings in Let Kyar, a deserted ethnic Rakhine village located in Mrauk U Township, which occurred on 16 May. Villagers in nearby Bu Ywat Ma Nyo had stated they heard gunshots and witnessed the Tatmadaw entering the village around the time the buildings were burnt. The military had stated earlier that it came into conflict with Arakan Army insurgents and blamed them for burning the village, though the latter denied it.[^cite_note-589] Witnesses alleged that the Tatmadaw burned down over 160 buildings of Hpayar Paung and Taung Pauk villages, located in Kyauktaw Township, on 3 September.[^cite_note-590] Amnesty International on 12 October reported that fires had been detected in the villages remotely with satellite sensors and the evidence pointed at Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-591]

The Buthidaung town was largely burnt, with Myanmar Team of the [Office of the High Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights) head James Rodehaver stating that they had received information that the arson began on 17 May, two days after the Arakan Army captured the town.[^cite_note-MayRohingya-435] The group however denied responsibility and blamed the Tatmadaw.[^cite_note-ByinePhyu-541]

### Rape

Myanmar security forces have reportedly raped Rohingya women during the violence. In February 2017, the UN's Human Rights Office published a report accused the army and police forces in Myanmar of committing gang rapes against Rohingya women.[^cite_note-newsweekReport-592][^cite_note-MyanmarDeniesRape-21] Systematic rape of Rohingya women has also been reported by [Amnesty International](18947898.md),[^cite_note-593] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md),[^cite_note-594] and other NGOs. [Reuters](18998750.md),[^cite_note-REUTERSrape-595] [IRIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Humanitarian),[^cite_note-IRINrape-596] [NPR](29697232.md),[^cite_note-NPRrape-597] [The Associated Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Associated_Press)[^cite_note-APrape-598] and the *[Myanmar Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_Times)*[^cite_note-JournalistFired-599] have all published reports of rape. Myanmar denies that there is a systematic campaign of rape, though acknowledges it may have been committed by "individual members of the security forces".[^cite_note-MyanmarDeniesRape-21]

Reports of rape began in October 2016. On 27 October, the *[Myanmar Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar_Times)* published an article reporting "dozens" of rapes in northern Rakhine state.[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-JournalistFired-599] The journalist who wrote that piece was later fired, allegedly due to government pressure.[^cite_note-JournalistFired-599] On 28 October, [Reuters](18998750.md) interviewed eight women who reported being raped by soldiers at gun point.[^cite_note-REUTERSrape-595] In April 2017, [National Public Radio](29697232.md) interviewed 12 women from different villages who all reported rape.[^cite_note-NPRrape-597] [IRIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Humanitarian) reported instances of rape in May 2017.[^cite_note-IRINrape-596]

Members of Myanmar's armed forces have been accused of raping civilians before.[^cite_note-NPRrape-597] In 2011, [Aung San Suu Kyi](2847.md) herself said "In the areas of the ethnic nationalities, rape is rife. It is used as a weapon by armed forces to intimidate the ethnic nationalities."[^cite_note-IRINrape-596] The United Nations' [Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Discrimination_against_Women) asked Myanmar on 28 November to provide reports on rapes, sexual violence and deaths of Rohingya women by their security forces and told them that action would be taken against the perpetrators.[^cite_note-601]

[United Nations Secretary General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Secretary_General) [António Guterres](471806.md) released a report on 16 April 2018 regarding conflict-related sexual violence specifically perpetrated by the Tatmadaw (Myanmar Armed Forces). In a [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) meeting, human rights activist Razia Sultana told a panel about the sexual violence in the conflict, based on research and interviews with Rohingyas she had conducted. Sultana claimed that there was evidence of soldiers raping more than 300 women and girls in 17 villages in Rakhine State. Hau Do Suan, Myanmar's representative to the United Nations, rejected the claims and stated that the report implicating the military was done based on unverified allegations.[^cite_note-602]

In September 2020, the Tatmadaw launched criminal proceedings against three soldiers who had gang-raped a woman and attempted to rape her daughter in Ugar village of Rathedaung Township on 29 June. The Tatmadaw had previously denied the accusations as false on 2 July.[^cite_note-603] All three were sentenced to 20 years in prison with hard labour in December.[^cite_note-604]

### Reactions

The military crackdown on Rohingya people drew criticism from various quarters. On 30 December 2016, nearly two dozen prominent human rights activists, including [Malala Yousafzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malala_Yousafzai), Archbishop [Desmond Tutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desmond_Tutu) and [Richard Branson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Branson), called on the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) to intervene and end the "ethnic cleansing and crimes against humanity" being perpetrated in northern Rakhine State.[^cite_note-CNN3-605]

High officials from the [United Nations](31769.md), and international organisations such as the human rights group [Amnesty International](18947898.md), have labelled the military crackdown on the Rohingya minority as "[crimes against humanity](61099.md)" and "[ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing)", adding that the military had made the civilians a target of "a systematic campaign of violence".[^cite_note-Aljazeera-humanity-62][^cite_note-606][^cite_note-607][^cite_note-BBC-Who-44] Amnesty International's Regional Director for Southeast Asia and the Pacific, James Gomez, said on 19 October 2017: "Aung San Suu Kyi today demonstrated that she and her government are still burying their heads in the sand over the horrors unfolding in Rakhine State. At times, her speech amounted to little more than a mix of untruths and [victim blaming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victim_blaming)."[^cite_note-608]

In August 2017, a government-appointed commission rejected allegations of human rights violations and [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing), calling them "exaggerations".[^cite_note-Jazeera2-609][^cite_note-Reuters5-610] In September 2017, Desmond Tutu wrote an open letter to [Aung San Suu Kyi](2847.md) urging her "to speak out for justice, human rights and the unity of your people".[^cite_note-Exodus-403][^cite_note-Ben-36]

Nobel Laureates Malala[^cite_note-why_wont_assk_act_2017_09_08_bbc-611] and Bishop Tutu again appealed to Aung San Suu Kyi to intercede on behalf of the Rohingya, and on 6 September 2017 [Pope Francis](1687680.md) called for an end to "persecution" of the Rohingya.[^cite_note-pope_laments_2017_08_27_ap_foxnews-93] On 14 September, U.S. Secretary of State Rex Tillerson said, "I think it is important that the global community speak out in support of what we all know the expectation is for the treatment of people regardless of their ethnicity... This violence must stop, this persecution must stop."[^cite_note-myanmar_faces_2017_09_14_ap_foxnews-612] [Prime Minister of the United Kingdom](24150.md) [Theresa May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theresa_May)'s spokesman announced on 19 September that the country would stop training Myanmar's armed forces until the crisis was resolved.[^cite_note-613]

Maung Maung Soe, who led the operations in Rakhine in 2017, was sanctioned by the United States according to the [Treasury Department](53667.md). It added that the US had "examined credible evidence of Maung Maung Soe's activities, including allegations against Burmese security forces of extrajudicial killings, sexual violence and arbitrary arrest as well as the widespread burning of villages."[^cite_note-614]

On 28 September 2018, the UNHCR voted to establish an international body to gather evidence of alleged atrocities committed by Myanmar's security forces and prepare files for future independent criminal proceedings.[^cite_note-615][^cite_note-616]

On 18 March 2019, Myanmar set up a military court to investigate alleged abuses.[^cite_note-617]

### Report by the OHCHR

On 11 October 2017, the [U.N. High Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.N._High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights) released a report titled the *Mission report of OHCHR rapid response mission to Cox's Bazar, Bangladesh*, which detailed the Burmese military's "systematic process" of driving away hundreds of thousands of Rohingyas from Myanmar. The report noted that prior to the attacks on 25 August 2017 and the military crackdown that ensued, the military pursued a strategy to:[^cite_note-ohchr_mission_report_2017_10_11_un-618][^cite_note-un_report_details_2017_10_11_reuters_guardian-619]

- have male Rohingyas between the ages of 15–40 years arrested and/or arbitrarily detained

- have Rohingya political, cultural and religious figures arrested and/or arbitrarily detained

- ensure that access to food, livelihoods and other means of conducting daily activities and life be taken away from Rohingya villagers

- drive out Rohingya villagers en masse through repeated acts of humiliation and violence, such as \[the] incitement of \[sectarian] hatred, violence and killings

- instill deep and widespread fear and trauma (physical, emotional and psychological) in Rohingyas, through acts of brutality; namely killings, disappearances, torture, and rape (and other forms of sexual violence)

## Government restrictions

Access to Rakhine State by journalists and observers has been restricted by the authorities.[^cite_note-620] Myanmar stated in mid-September that it does not bar aid workers from Rakhine, but authorities on ground might restrict access for security reasons.[^cite_note-Australian-583] [Doctors Without Borders](20498.md) urged Myanmar on 18 September to grant international humanitarian organisations unrestricted and independent access to the conflict zone. In a statement, the organisation said that its international staff had not been granted authorisation to visit the health facilities since the end of August, while the national staff was afraid to go work in the region, after Myanmar officials accused NGOs of colluding with ARSA.[^cite_note-621] In early October 2017, the United Nations denounced the government's refusal of access to Rakhine State, with [Mark Lowcock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Lowcock), the head of UN's humanitarian office, stating, "The access we have in northern Rakhine State is unacceptable."[^cite_note-622]

In June 2017, Myanmar's government refused to grant visas to a UN fact-finding mission appointed in March 2017.[^cite_note-623] In December 2017, Myanmar banned [Yanghee Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanghee_Lee), the United Nations' special rapporteur on the situation of human rights in Myanmar, who was due to visit the country in January 2018. The government stated that her ban was because of "biased and unfair" statements made by her during her visit in July 2017. Lee accused Aung San Suu Kyi of behaving similarly to the military juntas that ruled before her, citing her response to the treatment of Rohingya Muslims. Lee also stated that the decision suggested "something terribly awful" was happening in Rakhine State. The decision by Myanmar was criticised by Human Rights Watch and Amnesty International.[^cite_note-624][^cite_note-625]

A local newspaper had claimed that three days after two [Reuters](18998750.md) reporters were arrested on 12 December, five ethnic Rakhine villagers of Inn Din were arrested on suspicion of giving information about what happened in their village to journalists including the two arrested reporters according to some residents.[^cite_note-bbc.com-487] On 21 December, the UN rejected a report by *[The Irrawaddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Irrawaddy)* which claimed that the two journalists had passed information to them concerning the violence in Rakhine State. Reuters also denied they worked with the UN or any other organisation.[^cite_note-626] The [Ministry of Information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Information_\(Myanmar\)) cited the police as stating that the two were "arrested for possessing important and secret government documents related to Rakhine State and security forces". It added that they "illegally acquired information with the intention to share it with foreign media".[^cite_note-627] Reuters claimed that they were arrested for investigating a [mass grave](1349803.md) of Rohingyas near the village of [Inn Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_Din).[^cite_note-628]

Knut Ostby, UN's Acting Resident Coordinator and Humanitarian Coordinator for Myanmar, said in August that the UN was only permitted to work in a few villages and the officials often delayed requests to visit parts of Rakhine State.[^cite_note-629]

### 2019 internet shutdown

On 21 June 2019, the government of Myanmar authorised the shutdown of internet services in nine townships which include Ponnangyun, Kyauktaw, Maungdaw, Buthidaung, Rathedaung, Maruk U, Minbya, and Myebon townships in Rakhine State, as well as Paletwa township in [Chin State](493366.md).[^cite_note-630] In September 2019, internet restrictions were lifted in five of the nine townships; four in Rakhine State—Maungdaw, Buthidaung, Rathedaung, and Myebon—and Paletwa township in Chin State. However, they were reimposed in February 2020.[^cite_note-631]

On 22 February 2020, around 100 students gathered in Yangon and demanded an end to the internet shutdown. A case was filed by the government of Myanmar against the nine students who organised the protest, citing section 19 of the Peaceful Assembly Law, which outlaws unauthorised assemblies and carries a maximum six-month prison sentence.[^cite_note-632][^cite_note-633]

[Telenor Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telenor_Myanmar) announced on 3 February 2021 that internet restrictions had been lifted in the eight townships of Rakhine, bringing an end to the world's longest internet shutdown.[^cite_note-634]

## Misleading images

Misleading images have been used by both sides of the conflict, alongside claims of violence against civilians. Verifying the authenticity of images has become a challenge for researchers, due to media and travel restrictions imposed by Myanmar's government on Rakhine State. In 2016, an [OHCHR](22463.md) report stated that it would not use any photograph or video that it had not taken itself.[^cite_note-635] In August 2017, one of the images shared on [Twitter](9988187.md) by [Mehmet Şimşek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmet_%C5%9Eim%C5%9Fek), a [Deputy Prime Minister of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Turkey), was of the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md). He later deleted the image and issued a correction.[^cite_note-636]

In September 2017, Burmese officials shared photos that purportedly showed several Rohingyas setting fire to buildings in their own village. However, journalists later recognised two of the arsonists as Hindus from a nearby school building. The arsonists wore what appeared to be [tablecloths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tablecloth) on their heads to make themselves "appear Muslim". The [Eleven Media Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleven_Media_Group) published an article showing burnt Rohingya homes in Ka Nyin Tan, with government spokesman Zaw Htay tweeting a link to it with the caption "Photos of Bengalis setting fire to their houses!" After the story of the misleading images emerged, Zaw Htay said the government was investigating the matter.[^cite_note-637][^cite_note-638]

On 14 April 2018, Burmese authorities released photos of the "first [repatriated](1481067.md) Muslim family" receiving "National Verification Cards". [Bangladesh](3454.md)'s refugee commissioner, Abul Kalam, rejected Myanmar's claims of [repatriation](1481067.md), stating that the family never travelled beyond a no-man's land between the two countries.[^cite_note-639]

In July 2018, the Tatmadaw's department of public relations published a [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda) book titled "*Myanmar Politics and the Tatmadaw: Part I*", in which it contained photos purportedly showing the illegal immigration of Rohingyas during [British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_rule_in_Burma) and violence perpetrated by Rohingya villagers against [Rakhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakhine_people) villagers. It was later revealed by [Reuters](18998750.md) that the photos had been captioned misleadingly; a photo that supposedly showed a Rohingya man with the corpses of slain Rakhine locals was actually a photo taken during the [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War) of a man recovering the corpses of massacred [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengalis), and a photo that claimed to show the entry of hundreds of "Bengali intruders" (i.e. Rohingyas) into Rakhine State was in fact an award-winning photo of [Hutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutu) refugees taken in 1996. A third photo claiming to show Rohingyas entering Myanmar in a boat was actually of them leaving the country in 2015.[^cite_note-640][^cite_note-641] The publisher, a subsidiary of *[Myawady Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myawady_Daily)*, later issued an apology and stated that the photos were incorrectly published in the book.[^cite_note-642]

## International reactions

Main article: [International reactions to the Rohingya genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_Rohingya_genocide)

International condemnation of government and government-supported civilian attacks on Rohingyas and other minorities in the region led to widespread public protests around the world, in countries such as France and Indonesia.[^cite_note-protests_2017_09_08_cnn-643][^cite_note-horror_enraged_pakistanis_2017_09_12_washpost-644][^cite_note-myanmar_faces_2017_09_10_wsj-645]

The State Counsellor of Myanmar, [Aung Sun Suu Kyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aung_Sun_Suu_Kyi), has been criticised by the international community for not condemning the violence or the Burmese Army's military response to the crisis.[^cite_note-hundreds_dead_2017_09_10_washpost_chg_trib-97] Aung San Suu Kyi was initially backed by the Indian Prime Minister, [Narendra Modi](444222.md); however, the government of India quickly urged "restraint" in the "anti-terrorism" operations.[^cite_note-indian_pm_2017_09_06_cnn-646][^cite_note-india_urges_2017_09_10_wsj-647]

### Advisory Commission on Rakhine State

| \[icon] | This section **needs expansion** with: What is in the 2017 report. You can help by [adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Conflict_in_Rakhine_State_\(2016%E2%80%93present\)\&action=edit\&section=). *(September 2017)* |
| --- | --- |

In August 2016, former [UN Secretary-General](162415.md) [Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan) was invited to head the [Advisory Commission on Rakhine State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advisory_Commission_on_Rakhine_State), which was responsible for addressing human rights violations in the region.[^cite_note-648] The complete report was published a year later on 25 August – the same day new clashes erupted in the region[^cite_note-at_least_71_dead_2017_08_29_straits_times-91] – by the [Kofi Annan Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan_Foundation) and accepted by the government of Myanmar in August 2017,[^cite_note-649] in which it cited 10% of the world's stateless people as having originated from Rakhine State.[^cite_note-650] One of Kofi Annan's final statements after completing the commission's mandate was this:

> We propose a ministerial-level appointment to be made with the sole function of coordinating policy on Rakhine State and ensuring the effective implementation of the Rakhine Advisory Commission's recommendations. The appointee should be supported by a permanent and well-staffed secretariat, which will be an integral part of the Central Committee on Implementation of Peace and Development in Rakhine State and support its work.[^cite_note-651]

Retaliatory attacks by Myanmar's armed forces and Buddhist mobs ensued, leading to a further escalation in the regional humanitarian crisis.[^cite_note-what_is_going_on_2017_09_13_sydney_herald-652][^cite_note-new_committee_2017_09_13_ap_foxnews-653] Following the deaths of over a thousand civilians in the ensuing violence and the exodus of over 350,000 Rohingya refugees, the office of the President of Myanmar announced on 12 September that it had formed a new 15-member committee to implement the recommendations of the Rakhine Commission.[^cite_note-new_committee_2017_09_13_ap_foxnews-653] The President's Office stated that the *Implementation Committee of Rakhine Advisory Committee* was formed to adhere to the recommendations set for improving the security, social affairs and economic development of Myanmar's ethnic Rohingya Muslim minority, improving the sustainability of ethnic villages, removing camps for the displaced and accelerating the citizen verification process for Rohingyas under Myanmar's citizenship laws.[^cite_note-new_committee_2017_09_13_ap_foxnews-653]
